CIS Customs Chiefs Meet, Draft Customs
Alliance Accord

LD2311143694 Moscow Russian Television Network in
Russian 1100 GMT 23 Nov 94

[From the “Vesti” newscast]

[FBIS Translated Text] A routine session of the council
of heads of customs services of the CIS countries is
taking place in Moscow today.

[D. Maslennikov] This CIS body has drafted an agree-
ment on a custoi7s alliance and the principles of customs
legislation of the Commonwealth countries. In the view
of participants in today’s conference, a unified docu-
ment needed to be created to consolidate the main
principles of customs work within the framework of the
CIS. The document has defined the main principles of
legislation in the field, the status and functions of
customs bodies, has examined all issues relating to the
movement of goods and means of transport across the
borders of states and outlined custom regimes. The
law-enforcement aspect of the document which defines
customs bodies as investigative bodies also merits atten-
tion as it will help in fighting smuggling.

[V. Meshcheryakov, deputy chairman of the State Cus-
toms Committee of the Russian Federation] Over what
is almost the whole of 1994, goods have been seized at
crossing points worth a total of about 130 billion rubles
[R]. That is one thing. Second, we have also seized, or
rather prevented the export of goods which appeared
through some breach of the law worth a total of nearly
R160 billion. There have been and I believe there will be
other cases of, say, the smuggling of a container of
caesium. However, we discussed these problems with
our colleagues from European states. They said that
work needs to be done in this area and indeed both they
and we are working on it, but they also said that such
cases should not be overdramatized.

CIS Border Troops Commanders Meet on
‘Common Border’

MM2411142394 Moscow PRAVDA in Russian
24 Nov 94 p 1

[Report by Viktor Kharlamov: “One Border Common
To All. Meeting of CIS Border Troops Commanders
Held in Moscow”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] The session of the Council of CIS
Border Troops Commanders, which ended the other day
in Moscow, is yet another step along the road to a unified
system of protection for the Commonwealth’s external
borders. And as early as the beginning of next year a
treaty on guarding the external border, which will prob-
ably become a common border for all the CIS countries,
will be presented to heads of state to examine and then
sign.

At the end of the session I asked some of its participants
to give mini-interviews to PRAVDA. The question was
as follows:

INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS

“Why were border guards the first to reach mutual
understanding and start along the path of integration—
rather than politicians, the Armed Forces, or even law-
enforcement organs?”

This is how they replied.

Bolat Zakiyev, commander of the Republic of Kaza-
khstan Border Troops:

“It is simpler and more profitable to ensure our own
security in collaboration. After all, the security of each of
our states depends on the reliability of the Common-
wealth’s external border.”

Anarbek Bakayev, chairman of the Republic of Kaza-
khstan State Committee for National Security:

“‘We view our external borders as the common borders
of the Commonwealth. So that we, at least, have no
doubts as to the need for integration. I hope that the
process begun by the border guards will be supported by
other state and power structures.”

Valeriy Chkheidze, chairman of the Republic of Georgia
Department for State Border Security:

“Not all the newly-formed states have the capability or
the means to reliably secure their borders. This compels
us to pool our efforts to ensure our own security on our
external borders. Thus we are forced to integrate by the
very realities of life.”

Vasiliy Morkovkin, commander of the Republic of
Belarus Border Troops:

“There is an illegal transit of nonferrous and rare metals
to third countries through Belarus, as the Common-
wealth’s western gate. This is economically ruinous for
states such as Russia and Kazakhstan, and at the same
time it exacerbates the crime situation in our republic.
The same can be said of the illegal transit of weapons and
drugs. Thus we simply cannot do without collaboration
and coordinated actions.”

Anarey Nikolayev, commander in chief of the Russian
Feder: 10n Border Troops:

“The choice is as follows: Either each state of the
Commonwealth guards all its borders all the way along
its perimeter, spending colossal resources on equipping
new borders, or it takes the course of trust and mutual
commitments, leaving internal borders open and “trans-
parent“ and guarding the Commonwealth’s borders in
coordination and collaboration vth the other CIS states.
We have taken the second course, based on logic and
common sense.”

But the concept of the “border” does not just imply
Border Troops. It also means customs controls, which
ensure economic security, immigration control, counter-
intelligence, and phytosanitary services. In short, the
whole system.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

2 INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS

11 AR-TASS Carries Press Review for 24 Nov

LD2411071294 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 0659
GMT 24 Nov 94

[Press Review”—ITAR-TASS headline]

[FBIS Transcribed 1 ext] Moscow November 24 TASS—
The Russian press carries reports on President Boris
Yeltsin’s trip to Pskov region.

ROSSIISKAYA GAZETA quotes the president as saying
that the purpose of his trip was to see for himself how the
Russian-Estonian border has been equipped.

The president believes, the newspaper writes, that the
Russian-Estonian border is not quite a place where
everything is all right. But “this border has been, is and
will remain Russian one. We shall not cede an inch of
land under any circumstances whoever would lay claim
to it”.

The border with Estonia was sealed at the Helsinki
conference and, therefore, there can be no question of
any revision of it, the newspaper states.

NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA analyses the grain market
situation. The newspaper believes that next year may
become a year of grain shortages. A disastrous state of
affairs in the procurement of grain has developed in the
country, which is fraught with serious consequences for
urban dwellers.

The government, the newspaper maintains, has taken up
an ambigious stand over the matter—not to buy nation-
ally-produced grain and not to import any. This indi-
cates that the urban population may remain without
bread or, to be more exact, bread prices will soar.

IZVESTIYA comments on foreign trade liberalisation
problem, export quotas and licenses for energy
resources, in particular.

The newspaper believes that this is a principled issue
connected with a continuation of market-oriented
reforms. The newspaper points to inconsistency of the
authorities’ policy in this respect.

“The present flare-up of government discussion on the
export of oil is accounted for by at least two reasons:
firstly, the positions of the liberals in the government
have grown substantially stronger and, secondly, which
may be most importantly, that Russia expects to get
about 7,500 million U.S. dollars from international
financial organisations shortly.”

ROSSIISKIYE VESTI on a country-wide discussion of a
contemplated merger of two Russian television net-
works. The newspaper points out that “political and
mercantile considerations have come to prevail in the
discussion”.

“It strikes the eye that many of those who commented on
the intention expressed by the president to finally decide

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

the future of state-run television networks pursue prima-
rily their own private goals, not the main thign that
should have been discussed first—the interests of tele-
viewers”.

PRAVDA carries an interview with agrarian party leader
Mikhail Lapshin, who analyses the political situation in
the country in the light of his party’s policy.

“Sooner or later the great peasant country will have a
president who would represent the agrarian sector.
Russia has got the right to such a leader through suf-
fering, and peasantry as a political force is prepared to
nominate him”, Lapshin said.

NOVAYA YEZHEDNEVNAYA GAZETA (New Daily
Newspaper) on the cost of the repair of government and
parliament buildings.

The repair of the former residence of the parliament and
the state planning committee building which now houses
the state duna has cost 1,000 million U.S. dollars, a
competent soi rce in the government told the newspaper.

The newspape™ has estimated that the government and
parliament buildings repair cost was tantamount to that
of 200 houses for 170,000 people. “This would suffice to
resolve the housing problem of officers who are being
withdrawn from the Baltics,” the newspaper said.

NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA carries an article by former
Soviet president Mikhail Gorbachev who analyses the
U.S. policy with regard to Cuba. He believes that it is
time for the Americans to change their policy with regard
to the freedom island.

Gorbachev believes that the U.S. got an opportunity to
bring Fidel Castro to the negotiating table. Nevertheless,
the U.S. does not do that, which in itself is an evidence
of a striking political short-sightedness of the Wash-
ington administration, Gorbachev emphasises.

“In this case, just as in relations with new democracies in
Central Europe and with Russia, the U.S. continues to
cling tenaciously to Cold-War methods, demonstrating
its proud role and its haughtily assumed monopoly—to
be the spokesman for the aspirations of the free world”.

Press Review for 25 Nov

LD2511083694 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 0746
GMT 25 Nov 94

[Press Review”—ITAR-TASS headline]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow November 25 TASS—
A forecast for the socio-economic development of the
Russian Federation in 1995, the deterioration of the
criminogenic situation in the country and the covert
operation codenamed Sapphire to remove 600 kilo-
grarnmes of highly enriched uranium from Kazakhstan
to the United States are the main topics in today’s
Moscow-based newspapers.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI runs excerpts from the forecast
for the socio-economic development of the Russian
Federation in 1995, prepared by the Russian Ministry of
economics for an all-Russia economic conference
opening in the Kremlin on Saturday.

The document says in particular: “We are faced with a
difficult choice. The government considers it the only
reasonable option in the obtaining situation to come
forward with an active strategy for macroeconomic sta-
bilisation and the earliest economic recovery.”

“The main concept is to adopt a hard-and-fast budget
which would accurately reflect possibilities for the
mobilisation of revenue with due regard for the lessons
of the current year: reduce budget deficit down to
approximately eight per cent of gross domestic product,
to cover the budget deficit exclusively from non-
inflationary sources, i.e. The sale of government securi-
ties and borrowings from abroad”.

ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI reports that according to the
Russian Ministry of Labour, the incomes of 30.5 million
citizens in Russia in foreign currency equivalent is
approximately 42 dollars a month, with 20.5 per ceni of
people in Russia subsisting on such funds.

ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA gives data on a deterioration
of the criminogenic situation in the country. Referring to
interior ministry data, the newspaper reports that the
growth of crime in Russia doubled over the past four
years and that not less than 120,000 crimes are recorded
every month.

There were 2.8 million crimes last year, with 29,200 of
them being wilful murders. Criminal groups were in
control of up to 50,000 enterpreneureal entities and
more than 400 banks and ran thousands of enterprises
with the use of criminally-gotten capital. The share of
economic crimes, of which 15 were committed every
month, grew by more than 16 per cent within the year,
the newspaper noted.

ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA comments on a covert oper-
ation codenamed Sapphire to transfer 600 kg from
Kazakhstan to the United States.

Under existing arrangements between Moscow and ex-
Soviet republics, on the territories of which there remain

INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS 3

nuclear materials of military purpose following the
break-up of the Soviet Union, the materials should be
transferred to Russia to be processed at special plants
after an appropriate treatment into fuel ior nuclear
power stations.

IZVESTIYA on the uranium deal between Kazakhstan
and the U:S.:

“The main piquant thing is not even in Kazakhstan
people’s hard bargaining for every milligramme of the
material which they did not need at all. The peculiarity
which makes this story almost a Hollywood-style one is
that this almost ownerless uranium was found by Amer-
icans, not by Kazakhstan people.

IZVESTIYA, in another article on the same subject,
writes that “a Russian Foreign Ministry spokesman in
Moscow told us that Russia was informed in advance by
the American side about this purchase of uranium and
that there was an exchange of views on that score.
Moscow expressed confidence that the U.S. would
ensure a peaceful use of the material and would place it
under IAEA [International Atomic Energy Agency] safe-
guards. Washington promised to do everything note to
shake this confidence.”

TRUD carries an interview with Aman Tuleyev, deputy
to the Federation Council of Russia, who dwelt mainly
on the position of coalminers.

Touching upon the problems of inter-ethnic relations,
Tuleyev emphasised that “ordinary people in Kaza-
khstan, Ukraine and Belarus (I do not mention other
republics so far) obviously gravitate towards closer rela-
tions with Russia, and the for many reasons which
include centuries-old mutual interests, inter-ethnic mar-
riages, and the neighbouring peoples’ cultural and eco-
nomic relations which closely intertwined.”

“I am confident that sooner or later the peoples of at
least these four countries will make politicians realise
that none of ordinary people need the ‘transparent’
borders between them. The borders hamper contacts,
and, which is the main thing, hinder efforts to get out of
the common economic crisis. A politician who would not
heed this voice of the people is doomed by history to
oblivion and curse,” Tuleyev said.

Yeltsin Suspends Limitation Measures on
Yugoslavia

LD2311174594 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1534
GMT 23 Nov 94

[From the “Diplomatic Panorama” feature by diplo-
matic correspondents Aleksandr Korzun, Igor Porshnev,
Yevgeniy Terekhov and others]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] The president’s press service
announced on Wednesday that Yeltsin had passed a
directive documenting the suspension of a number of
limitation measures in relation to Yugoslavia.

For instance, the ban has been lifted on the Russian
planes’ flights to Belgrade in accordance with the U.N.
Security Council’s Resolution No. 943 of September 23,
1994.

“Yeltsin has brought the Russian laws in line with the
international law,” a high-ranking Russian diplomat told
Interfax.

He said that the ban on the flights of Russian planes to
Belgrade and other limitations were lifted back on
October 5, because Resolution No. 943 went into effect
immediately.

Shumeyko Says NATO Bombing Beyond

*K easonable Limits’

LL2511095894 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 0803
GM1 25 Nov 94

‘By ITAR-TASS special correspondent Sergey Sereda]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Lisbon November 25 TASS—
Russian Federation Council Speaker Vladimir Shum-
eyko believes the latest NATO air strikes on Bosnian
Serbs are an excessively severe measure and insists that
the conflict in Bosnia should be settled only by peaceful
means.

“We shall support a compromise by all means and will
remember that the means should be 99 percent peace-
ful’’, he told Portuguese lawmakers during a meeting on
Thursday.

The NATO bombings “are a measure which is beyond
the necessary reasonable limits. I can say that it is an
excessive measure”, the chairman of the upper chamber
of the Russian parliament said.

He added that it is inadmissible to side with any party
involved in the Bosnian conflict and warned that the
lifting of the arms embargo on Bosnian muslims can flare
up the conflict as weapons deliveries allow to earn
enormous profits.

“Military assistance to any of the conflicting parties is
inadmissible. If there is an embargo it should be strictly

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

observed, if it is lifted it should be lifted from every-
body”, Shumeyko said and stressed that the lifting of the
embargo would result “in direct material profits earned
on the conflict”.

“Russia proceeds from the principle foreign policy posi-
tion that it is categorically inadmissible to side with any
of the conflicting parties”, he stressed.

Comments on World Model, Foreign Policy

LD2511095294 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in
Russian 1750 GMT 24 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS special correspondent Sergey Sereda]

[FBIS Translated Excerpt] Lisbon, 24 Nov— A conver-
sation today between members of the Portuguese parlia-
mentary commission on foreign affairs and a delegation
from Russia’s Federation Council lasted twice as long as
planned. The delegation, which is here on an official
visit, is led by Federation Council Speaker Vladimir
Shumeyko. [passage omitted]

In the discussion on international topics, Vladimir
Shumeyko said that Russia supports the multipolar
model of the world, replacing the bipolar model which
ceased to exist when the Soviet Union disintegrated.
Russia will never agree with the unipolar model of the
world in which the United States is interested. Russia is
currently making a general reassessment of its relations
with many countries, rejecting the division of the world
into “first”, “second” and “third”. [passage omitted]

The Portuguese MP’s asked a series of questions linked
with the Yugoslav conflict. Russia’s position, Vladimir
Shumeyko said, is based on the principal postulate of our
country’s foreign policy: in a conflict in which two or
more sides are taking part, it is categorically wrong to
take the side of any one of them. We will support efforts
to find a compromise by any means, bearing in mind
that the means must be 99 percent peaceful. This makes
military aid to one of the conflicting sides all the more
unacceptable. If there is an embargo, then it should be
strictly implemented, and if it is lifted, then it should be
lifted on all sides. The lifting of an embargo on arms
trading leads to a sharp rise in arms prices and the
derivation of direct material profit f:om the conflict. As
for the recent bombings, it should be understood that
this measure exceeds necessary and reasonable limits.
Such measures can be described as redundant.

Chernomyrdin Interviewed on Results of Visit to
UAE

LD2311143894 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1329
GMT 23 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondents Gennadiy Yezhov and
Vyacheslav Anchikov]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Abu Dhabi November 23
TASS—“I am satisfied with the results of the visit to the

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

United Arab Emirates. The talks conducted by the
Russian delegation here were constructive and useful,”
Russian Prime Minister Viktor Chernomyrdin said in an
interview with the local WAM news agency on
Wednesday.

“We discussed an entire range of various aspects of
cooperation at the talks with UAE Deputy Prime Min-
ister [and President] Shaykh Sultan Bin-Zayid al
Nuhayyan and meetings with Abu Dhabi Crown Prince
Shaykh Khalifah Bin-Zayid al Nuhayyan and other
leaders of the country.

“I'd like to state that we met understanding on all
problems under discussion,” the Russian Cabinet head
said.

“In actual fact,” the premier noted, “we only open the
UAE for ourselves, and this visit should pave grounds
for the start of fruitful cooperation between the two
states.”

In this connection, Chernomyrdin expressed confidence
that the new agreement on trade, economic and scientific
cooperation between Russia and the UAE will be signed
in the near future.

“We are also preparing for signing an agreement to
protect mutual investments, to avoid double taxation
and other documents,” he continued.

The premier stressed that Russia offers foreign partners
good possibilities to establish joint ventures and to
participate in financing various projects, especially if
one takes into account the on-going conversion of the
munitions industry in Russia.

“Russia has rich experience in the oil and gas indus-
tries,” he added. All this can become spheres of bilateral
cooperation. Chernomyrdin called on UAE businessmen
to go on a fact-finding visit to Russia and to learn
possibilities opening for them.

Speaking about the discussion of foreign policy issues
with the UAE leaders, including the situation in the
Gulf, the premier stressed that economic cooperation
between states is a major factor for achieving stability,
including in this region.

In conclusion, Chernomyrdin shared his impression of
what he saw in Abu Dhabi. What has been done in this
country oniv for 20 years, he said, evokes fascination,
and the great merit for this goes to UAE President
Shaykh Zayid bin-Sultan al Nuhayyan.

[By ITAR-TASS diplomatic correspondent Andrey
Shtorkh]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow November 23 TASS—
Russia expressed concern over a new air strike by NATO
on a Serb missile base in western Bosnia on Wednesday.

“The new alarming reports from Bosnia caused our
serious concern”, spokesman of the Hussian Foreign
Ministry Grigoriy Karasin told TASS.

He added that details of the new air st.ike and of the
general military activities in Bosnia are yet to be atten-
tively analysed. ‘““However, the danger of a chain reac-
tion of power methods is already clear and, according to

bilateral basis.

Recall of Russian Peacekeepers Said Possible

LD2311171394 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1702
GMT 23 Nov 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Russia is “extremely con-
cerned” about the emerging trend toward resolving

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

Bosnian settlement issues exclusively by force, a high-
ranking member of Russia’s Foreign Ministry told
INTERFAX on Wednesday in comments on the report
concerning another NATO air strike on Bosnian Serbs’
positions in the area of Otok north-east of Bihac.

“There is no doubt that the Bosnian Serbs, who had fired
at the NATO planes engaged in patrolling the ‘no fly
zone’, provoked the air strike. However another thing is
noteworthy: the international community is gradually
sliding toward supporting militarily only one of the
parties involved in the conflict,” he pointed out.

According to the diplomat, this may lead to the start of a
full-scale war in the Balkans. “The use of force may
provoke a tough reaction both from the Bosnian Serbs
and from Belgrade, which has restrained itself so far, but
can lose control ver itself some time,” he said.

As the diplomat said, under the circumstances Russia
may decide to recall its peacekeepers from the former
Yugoslavia.

“We are no suicides, and we shall not sacrifice our
people. It would be a crime against them,” he stressed.

The diplomat declined to say when the decision to recall
Russian peacekeepers will be taken. “If at its meeting to
be held on the Ie vel of foreign ministers in Brussels on
December 2 the Contact Group fails to curb the trend
toward resolving all issues by force, Moscow will take its
decision promptly. It would be deadly to delay it,” he
concluded.

Kozyrev Holds News Conference on Bosnia

LD2411152794 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in
Russian 1427 GMT 24 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS diplomatic correspondents Andrey
Serov and Andrey Shtorkh]

{[FBIS Translated Text] Moscow, 24 November—
Foreign Minister of the Russian Federation Andrey
Kozyrev has warned about the danger of “third forces”
being drawn into the Bosnian conflict. At a meeting with
journalists today he voiced the opinion that the Muslim
side was deliberately provoking the Serbs in the hope of
NATO strikes at the latter in response.

“The Muslim side conducted the latest offensive obvi-
ously calculating that it would succeed in dragging
NATO, outside third forces, into the conflict on its side,
persuade people to lift the embargo on deliveries of arms
and, most important of course, secure air strikes,” the
head of the Russian foreign policy department said.

At the same time, he noted, the main reason for the
continuing confrontation is the Bosnian Serbs’ not
agreeing to accept the peace settlement plan proposed by
the “contact group.”

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

The Bosnian and Krajina Serbs, A.Kozyrev indicated,
are responding without restraint to the Muslims’ provo-
cations, going outside “the reasonable framework of
response” and “stepping every time on the same rakes.”
As a result, the minister said, there is at present a danger
of “‘a third force” being drawn into the armed forces. The
“unrestrained escalation” now taking place had become
an object of the concern of Russian diplomacy.

“We would like to halt the logic of the war, and con-
nected with this are our initiatives in the ‘Contact
Group’, a session of which at the level of foreign minis-
ters will be held at the beginning of December,” the
minister said. “I hope that the meeting of the ‘Contact
Group’ will send a clear signal that the international
community is not for escalation of military confronta-
tion but for a return to political methods.”

Replying to an ITAR-TASS correspondent’s question,
the minister said that at this meeting it is planned to
discuss a number of proposals, conditionally pooled
together under the title ‘Plan B’. The idea, he said, is to
present, in addition to the ‘Geneva package’, a second
plan which would exist paraliel to the first one.
According to the authors of ‘Plan B’, it is due to help get
out of the deadlock which was formed following the
Bosnian Serbs’ refusal to adopt the “Geneva package’.
This is an attempt to find the ‘next best’ way around it,
to begin tackling other problems, and then to go back to
solving the central question. “We believe that ‘Plan B’
contains the basis for discussion; it has elements worthy
of attention and elements which give rise to very serious
doubts”, A.Kozyrev said.

Churkin on Possible Pullout From Yugoslavia

MM2311200794 Moscow KOMSOMOLSKAYA
PRAVDA in Russian 24 Nov 94 p 1

[Interview with Vitaliy Churkin, Russian ambassador to
Brussels, by Ravil Zaripov under “Firsthand” rubric;
date, place not given: “Russian ‘Blue Helmets’ May
Leave Former Yugoslavia, Vitaliy Churkin Toid Us”’—
first paragraph is introduction]

{[FBIS Translated Text] We asked Vitaliy Churkin, for-
merly the Russian president's special representative for
the settlement of the Yugoslav crisis, who recently took
up the post of Russian ambassador to Brussels, to
comment on the situation that has developed since the
NATO military action in Serb Krajina.

{Churkin] I would like to remind you that NATO has a
very limited remit in Bosnia and operates in accordance
with the UN Security Council resolutions. You can argue
about the details but, strictly speaking. picking bones
with NATO does not work today since the UN Security
Council did give authorization. The NATO Council
adopted the corresponding decisions and immediately
briefed our embassy on this as the embassy of a perma-
nent Security Council member country and one of the
main participants in the settlement of the conflict.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL A¥FAIRS 7

[Zaripov] We have information that the United States
intends to train Bosnian troops and that even now
specialists are at work there. What then is the point of
the Contact Group’s activity?

{[Churkin] A tremendous U.S.-European and even intra-
NATO furor has blown up over this here in Brussels. But
at the moment the Americans are categorically denying
that anything is being done in this area. If the report is
confirmed, this will not only undermine NATO unity
but the Contact Group as well.

[Zaripov] How do you assess the activity of tne UN
forces? Maybe it would be better to send the “blue
helmets” home and give the warring parties themselves
the opportunity to achieve reconciliation?

[Churkin] Officially the UN mission is not to .concile
the sides but to provide the civilian population with
humanitarian aid. At this moment withdrawing the UN
troops would not be the best option. If it does all boil
down to the sides’ unwillingness to make peace and
insisting on the unchecked escalation of the conflict then
of course the troops should leave. But in that case the
conflicting parties and those who are helping them to
continue the war by advocating the lifting of the embargo
on arms shipments must bear the responsibility for
everything.

[Zaripov] If the United States does nonetheless unilater-
ally lift the embargo on arms shipments to the Muslims,
what should Russia’s response be, in your view?.

[Churkin] In this case we should withdraw our UN
battalion. And Foreign Minister Andrey Kozyrev has
stated that publicly. The British and French are ready to
act similarly with their contingents. I see this as another
component of the European countries’ joint stance.

Report on Ambassador Pickering’s Visit to Duma

MM2511093794 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
25 Nov 94 p 5

[Report by Sergey Chugayev: “U.S. Ambassador Prom-
ises To Improve Visa Service for Russian Citizens”’]

{FBiS Translated Text] The Consular Department of the
U.S. Embassy in Russia plans to make substantial
changes to its activity with a view to speeding up and
simplifying the procedure for issuing visas to Russian
citizens wishing to visit the United States. In particular,
it is intended to employ the practice of written responses
to questions instead of the mandatory interview with
Consular Service personnel. U.S. Ambassador to Russia
Pickering told your IZVESTIYA correspondent this fo!-
lowing a conversation that he had at the State Duma
with S. Yushenkov, chairman of the Duma Defense
Committee.

Mr. Pickering also announced plans to expand the Con-
sulate’s premises in the hope that Russians waiting for
visas will not freeze while waiting in line on the street.
The Embassy has met with support from Moscow Mayor
Yu. Luzhkov in resolving this problem, but everything is
being held up by the sluggishness of the Russian Admin-
istration for Servicing the Diplomatic Corps, the ambas-
sador said.

A number of recent moves by leading Russian parlia-
mentarians in protest at the actions of the U.S. Consular
Service in Moscow were the reason for the head of the
U.S. Embassy’s visit to the Duma. Thus, after P. Grig-
orenko, the son of the well-known rights defender, who
wanted to visit his sick mother, was denied a visa,
Human Rights Commissioner S. Kovalev said that he no
longer wants to visit the United States. His example was
soon followed by V. Lukin, chairman of the Duma
Committee for International Affairs, and S. Yushenkov,
chairman of the Duma Defense Committee. They aban-
doned scheduled U.S. visits and said that they are
suspending all contacts with U.S. citizens.

This was how the heads of the two Duma committees
responded to the fact that the U.S. State Department
adopted a decision at the highest level to issue V.
Zhirinovskiy with a visa at a time when the Duma is
receiving numerous complaints from Russian citizens
regarding the rudeness of U.S. consular officials and
unwarranted refusals to grant visas (not so long ago, for

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

instance, a group of Russian children, mainly from
children’s homes and boarding schools—-winners of the
“Glass Slipper” Music Contest—werve subjected to
humiliating checks, which by no means everyone
passed). (Pickering confirmed that the LDPR [Liberal-
Democratic Party of Russia] leader was issued with a
visa on the personal orders of the U.S. secretary of state).

The activity of the U.S. Embassy Consular Department
has long since come to the attention of the Russian press.
It is hard not to notice the long line of people standing
outside the building on Sadovoye Koltso in all weathers.
The Russian Foreign Ministry, parliament, and mass
media editorial offices constantly receive complaints
from our fellow citizens regarding what they consider to
be the discrimination of Consulate officials and their
insulting behavior. According to the Russian Foreign
Ministry, 400-500 people a day come to the Embassy’s
Consular Department. And between 20 and 25 percent
of the visa applicants are turned down.

The promptness with which the U.S. ambassador
responded to the clash by visiting the State Duma is
evidence of his concern at the situation that has devel-
oped. However, as I learned from the participants in the
meeting, the ambassador by no means came empty-
handed. According to Pickering, around 15 percent of
the Russian citizens who receive short-term visas stay in
the United States for good. And that forces Consulate
officials to display particular vigilance. At times beyond
what is reasonable. He promised to conduct an official
investigation into the officials who have been the cause
of complaints. What is more, it turns out that U.S.
official organs are also receiving plenty of complaints
from U.£. citizens who have clashed with our consular
officials when obtaining Russian entry visas—they com-
plain of slowness and are puzzled why the Russian
Consulate requires an additional fee for speeding up the
issuing of visas.

Ambassador Pickering promised Yushenkov that he
would petition the U.S. government to unilaterally intro-
duce a more liberal procedure when it comes to pro-
cessing Russian citizens’ entry to this country. The
interlocutors also agreed to do everything they can to
promote the conclusion of a consular agreement between
Russia and the United States.

At the end of the meeting S. Yushenkov told journalists
that he is pleased with the results and is suspending his
boycott of the United States. There is every indication
that V. Lukin will do the same on his return from the
Czech Republic.

U.S. Congress Changes Not Mandate for
‘Foolhardiness’

MM 2511091394 Moscow TRUD in Russian 24 Nov 94
po6

[Article by correspondent Vissarion Sisnev: “Sunday
Does not Come After Tuesday, Specially When It’s Black
Tuesday”’]

[FBIS Translated Excerpt] Washington—For all the talk
in the runup to the midterm elections on 8 November
that voters were “‘angrier than ever before,” only zbout
38 percent of the electorate actually turned out to vote,
that is to say, roughly 75 million people voted while
around 113 million stayed home. Although this is the
best result at such elections since 1982, only by
stretching a point can this vote be described as an
expression of the popular will.

However its outcome, I would remind you, ended the
Democratic Party’s 40-year-long domination of Con-
gress, while at the same time not a single Republican lost
his seat. [passage omitted]

It is essential that the president do something special if
he wants to stay on for a second term. The presidential
staff are reorganizing the work program, including legis-
lative initiatives, in line with the new configuration of
forces. In particular, according to the magazine U.S.
NEWS AND WORLD REPORT, Clinton “plans to
focus more than ever before on international relations,
where his presidential powers are very considerable.” It
goes on to say that “unpleasantnesses may iie ahead for
him.” Russia is acting increasingly tough toward the
former Soviet republics and is issuing a challenge to U.S.
policy vis-a-vis Yugoslavia, Iraq, and Iran and the
admission of certain former USSR satellites to NATO.
In response the administration is abandoning the ‘Russia
first’ rule.“

Does this mean that matters are heading toward a
worsening, if not an exacerbation, of American-Russian
relations. After all, Moscow too does not rule out that
this is possible with the advent of the Republicans to

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 9

power. To my question about this, an official whose post
makes him very familiar with the problem answered as
follows on condition that his anonymity be preserved:
“It must not be forgotten that the White House’s present
policy did not come about in a vacuum, the foundations
of American-Russian relations were laid under the
Republicans, Reagan and Bush. But they develop favor-
ably only when there are joint efforts on both sides.”

That is true, but even Moscow can understand when it
hears that the most likely chairman of the Senate Foreigr
Relations Committee, the right-wing Republican from
North Carolina Jesse Helms, and kindred spirits are
accusing the Clinton administration of “having been too
soft on Russia since the outset” and saying that instead
of troops being sent to Haiti they should have been sent
to Cuba to overthrow Castro. A chill runs down one’s
spine at such statements. And oil has also been poured
on the flames by certain observers like, for example,
ex-diplomat George Kennan, who wrote in the conser-
vative WASHINGTON TIMES: “Clinton’s Russian
friends are the same Russians who are seeking to expand
the antidemocratic Russian empire again.”

A now-familiar note is heard in such statements: We
thought that the Russians would henceforth act only as
we wished, but they.... Such a note did creep in even
before, prior to the Republicans’ triumph, under the oid
regime, as it were, including in statements by its repre-
sentatives. It has to be hoped that this “disappointment”
will not poison our relations with America and that
Washington won't take up or, more accurately, revive
the fashion of showing “toughness toward the disobe-
dient Russians” implied in commentaries about “Amer-
icans drifting toward the right.” The mandate on Black
Tuesday was given for honesty, level-headedness, and
farsightedness, not for foolhardiness and reckless talk.

LD2411191094 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1701
GMT 24 Nov 94

[From the “Diplomatic Panorama” feature: By diplo-
matic correspondents Aleksandr Korzun, Igor Porshnev,
Yevgeniy Terekhov, and others]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Russian Foreign Minister
Andrey Kozyrev has said he would like the future CSCE
Budapest summit on December 5-6 to make decisions
that would enable this organization to “tackle such
major issues as, for instance, the settlement in Nagorno-
Karabakh.”

“We support the CSCE’s stronger peacekeeping capabil-
ities. The world is entering a situation when there will be
quite possible real conflicts, not only that in Yugoslavia,
rather than euphoria about an end to the East-West
confrontation,” he pointed out.

According to Kozyrev, “now Russia’s partners are
starting to think why not to carry out a CSCE operation
in Nagorno-Karabakh considering that the U.N. is

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

10 RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

already overloaded and there is reluctance to launch a
U.N. operation.” “However, everyone proves to himself
that the CSCE is simply unprepared for the task: there is
no mechanism, financing, etc.,” he continued.

Kozyrev said Moscow had “managed to convince its
partners that the CSCE should not be thrown away as a
cut-off slice.”

“Both in the West and Central Europe there is what I call
NATO centrism, NATO mania. Supporters of these
ideas insist that the CSCE has exhausted itself and only

have managed to break the spine to such arguments,” he
emphasized.

UN Representative Lavrov Interviewed

MM 2511110394 Moscow ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI in
Russian 23 Nov 94 p 3

{Interview in New York with Sergey Lavrov, Russian
Federation permanent representative to the United
Nations, by Ilya Baranikas; date not given: “Instruc-
tions—To Protect the Interests of the Motherland”]}

[FBIS Translated Text] Sergey Lavrov, Russian Federa-
tion permanent representative to the United Nations,
has recently started work in New York. He had been
Russian Federation deputy foreign minister since 1992.
Our correspondent in the United States, Ilya Baranikas,
spoke to him.

[Baranikas] You worked in the Russian missior at the
United Nations back in the years when it was the
mission of the Soviet Union. Has the policy which the
United States has been conducting in the United Nations
changed in the years of your absence in New York?

[Lavrov] Yes, to some extent it has changed, of course:
During my first posting there, Reagan’s Republican
administration was in power, whereas now the Demo-
crats are occupying the White House. The Republicans
only realized the need to use the United Nations more
actively in the interests of their own foreign policy
toward the end of their time in power, when the war in
the Persian Gulf was beginning. But the Democrats were
already speaking about it during the phase of Clinton’s
election campaign.

Today American diplomacy is operating very forcefully
in the United Nations. I believe that this is right: A
country which wants to protect its interests should be
energetic. Although at the same time it should also be
respectful toward others. We also try to operate like this.
It does not always work. But that is entirely natural: I
think that you can scarcely find any country’s mission at
the United Nations which carries out all instructions
from its capital successfully.

[Baranikas] During the recent vote in the General
Assembly on the question of removing the embargo on
arms supplies to Bosnia, it is said that Madame Albright,

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

U.S. permanent representative to the United Nations,
walkcd around the hall persuading delegates from var-
ious countries to vote for this resolution. Is this a normal
phenomenon?

[Lavrov] Yes, entirely normal: After all. she was cam-
paigning for this during the discussion, before the vote. If
a question is of fundamental significance to your
country, then such actions are entirely justified.

[Baranikas] And as Russia’s permanent representative,
would you act in the same way if the need arose?

[Lavrov] Yes, of course, if it concerned an issue of great
importance to my country.

[Baranikas] During the vote on the Bosnian resolution,
the Russian delegation abstained. The resolution was
adopted by a majority of votes, but it is predicted that it
will be rejected by the Security Council...

[Lavrov] Of the 15 countries that belong to the Security
Council, nine abstained during the vote at the General
Assembly session, so the outcome of the vote in the
Security Council can largely be predicted. Not just
Russia, but a number of other countries are convinced
that opening channels for supplying weapons to Bosnia
will only add fuel the fire of internecine strife and cannot
bring a political settlement to the conflict nearer.

[Baranikas] In New York, apart from Russia’s perma-
nent mission to the United States, there is also the
Russian Federation Consulate General. People who are
far-removed from international politics sometimes find
it hard to see the difference between them.

[Lavrov] We represent Russia’s interests in the United
Nations, while the Consulate General concerns itself
with bilateral U.S.-Russian relations as applied to New
York. If, for instance, a government or parliamentary
delegation arrives from Russia for bilateral talks with the
Americans, then the program for its stay is prepared by
the Consulate General; it provides the delegation with
transportation, reserves hotels, and so on. But if some
Russian representatives arrive in order to participate in
UN events, then the Russian Permanent Mission at the
United Nations deals with them. Consular matters
(visas, passports, and other things) come under the
sphere of activity of the Consulate General. Ties with
compatriots abroad are also formally its business. But we
do not stand aloof either. An exhibition of sculptures by
Ernst Neizvestnyy was organized in the permanent mis-
sion building this year, and now you can see Mikhail
Shemyakin’s pictures there... If any Russians living in
America appeal to us with their problems, we do not pass
the buck, we try to help.

[Baranikas] What problems connected with the United
Nations does Russia consider to be a priority?

[Lavrov] Today our country is paying particular atten-
tion to two spheres in the activity of the United
Nations—peacekeeping and socioeconomic activity.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

Peacekeeping primarily because we need stability on the
perimeter of our borders. We are interested in stability in
more distant areas of the world too. It would be good if
less money was spent on UN peacekeeping operations. A
tota! of $3.5 billion a year on peacekeeping forces is an
enormous sum. And furthermore these operations are
not always effective—look at Somalia... Incidentally, we
expressed doubts when that operation was only just
beginning. But our position was not supported at that
time.

The second group of priorities is Russia's economic
interests. We are interested in making the programs
adopted by the General Assembly and the UN Economic
and Social Council, which envisage UN support to
countries with a transitional economy, more specific.
One of the UN projects for Russia, and there is a whole
series of them, concerns the development of democratic
institutions and administrative structures. This project
is being elaborated with the participation of Russian
government departments, the parliament, and the
regions.

We are also trying to reduce Russia’s expenditure on
participation in the United Nations—contributions into
its regular budget and particularly for peacekeeping
operations. Our payments have already been reduced as
compared with the USSR’s contributions. The UN Com-
mittee on Contributions has made a recommendation on
the subsequent reduction of our financial participation
to 4.5 percent of the UN budget.

[Baranikas] What about Russia’s debt to the United
Nations, which constitutes $600 million?

[Lavrov] We are paying it off gradually. In the near
future we are waiting for a decision from Moscow on the
schedule for paying off debts. Things are difficult for
Russia now, but these are not debts which can be put off
till better times.

[Baranikas] You are not a new recruit in the United
Nations. What is the main difference in your present
working conditions and working conditions in former
times?

[Lavrov] It is true that I worked here 1981 through 1988
in the mission’s economic department as first secretary,
aide, senior aide, and then head of the economic advi-
sory board. And before New York, I was in the USSR
Foreign Ministry International Economic Organizations
Department.

This time I am going to have to be involved not only in
economics, but also in all international problems: In my
post I represent the country in the United Nations
Security Council, the General Assembly, the Economic
and Social Ceuncil, and other UN organs which meet in
session in New York. The lion’s share of the work will be
taken up by work in the Security Council.

As far as any changes in the working conditions are
concerned, then these are fairly substantial and have
made our lives perceptibly easier.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 11

A great many contrived restrictions, which used to
regulate the activity of and visits by our diplomats ficre,
have been abolished. Every staffer at the Russian mis-
sion is now free to decide for himself how and where he
will spend his weekend or vacation. Russians who work
abroad can travel to third countries. You can now invite
relatives from Russia with no restrictions: In the past,
only husbands, wives, and minors were allowed to visit.
We can now send our children to American schools,
which was banned in the past.

[Baranikas] Do you remember how the Soviet colony’s
primary party organization, which for some reason was
disguised and called a “trade union” organization, used
to operate?

[Lavrov] Yes, and the trade union organization was
conspiratorially called a “local committee,” while the
Communist Youth League was called a “sports” organi-
zation... These absurdities no longer exist, just as UN
specialists who are Russian citizens and work in a
personal capacity in various UN organs no longer have
to “voluntarily” give up part of their earnings. In the
past their wages were “trimmed” to the general Soviet
level, and they were made equal in material terms with
Soviet diplomats at various levels. All this has disap-
peared without a ‘race, but certain restrictions still exist
on the American side.

[Baranikas] For example?

[Lavrov] In the past all Soviet diplomats had to inform
the U.S. authorities three days in advance if they were
going to leave the 25-mile (40 km) zone around the city
center. This procedure was supposed to disappear in
accordance with an agreement between the United States
and Russia. But for the time being, these restrictions are
only removed for diplomats of aide rank and higher.
There is no logic to be found here.

Japan Postpones Repayment of Russian Debts

LD2511121894 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1141
GMT 25 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS diplomatic correspondent Nikolay Ger-
onin]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow November 25 TASS—
The Russian side has had talks with Japanese represen-
tatives to postpone the repayment of Russian debts, and
Japan, as member of the “Paris Club”, has decided to
satisfy this request. Tokyo has made this decision in
keeping with the agreement reached within the frame-
works of the “Paris Club” last June to sign by November
30 all the necessary bilateral documents with Russia,
putting off the repayment of its debts, ITAR-TASS was
told today at the Russian Finance Ministry. The corre-
sponding Russo-Japanese agreement is to be signed
together with other documents during Russian Vice-
Premier Oleg Soskovets’ upcoming visit to Japan, sched-
uled for November 27.

Japanese officials have announced in Tokyo on
Thursday that the Japanese Government had decided to
put off the repayment of Russian debts to Japanese trade
companies, amounting to 180 million dollars, which had
to be paid back in 1994. Moscow will apparently have to
begin repaying this sum two years and nine months from
now, and to complete it within a period of thirteen years.

Russia Involved in Japanese Space Rocket Project

LD2411163694 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1421
GMT 24 Nov 94

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] The Japanese space rocket
M-3S2 will put in space a ballistic research capsule of
Russian manufacture early next year. The “Express”
capsule containing research instruments of the Japanese
Space and Astronautics Institute and the German space
agency Deutsche Aerospace AG is to be launched in
mid-January.

The Express project (Experimental Re-entry Space
System) is being carried out under a bilateral German-
Japanese space cooperation agreement and among other
things, will probe into the effects of micro-gravitation on
various systems.

Under the agreement the sides divided duties - Germany
undertook to provide a capsule by signing a DM12-
million-contract (about 7.5 million dollars) with the
Khrunichev State Research-Industrial Center in Russia,
and Japan - to put the capsule in space with the M-3S2
rocket of the Japanese space and astronautics institute.

The Japanese rocket will put the capsule in an elliptic
orbit between 270 kilometers (perigee) and 380 kilome-
ters (apogee). After a series of experiments in orbit the
capsule will return to Earth near town of Woomera, in
Australia and then be moved to Bremen, Germany, for
data analysis.

In case the Russian capsule is used successfully the
Khrunichev industrial center will be commissioned to
make an Express-2 for the European rocket Ariane-5.

Soskovets Visit to Japan Previewed

MM2511102994 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
25 Nov 94 p 3

[Report by Sergey Agafonov: “Soskovets Going to Japan
as Chief Economist’”’]

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 13

[FBIS Translated Text] Tokyo—Thanks to the efficiency
and caustic attitude of Japanese reporters—who enjoy
copious news leaks from official Japanese depart-
ments—the veil of secrecy covering virtually everything
about the forthcoming visit to Tokyo by a Russian
Government superdelegation headed by Oleg Soskovets
has been lifted.

The Japanese general public are already aware that the
culmination of the visit will be the signing of a joint
document and the institution a new structure with the
high-sounding name “Intergovernmental Commission
for Trade and Economic Issues”; Soskovets will be
asking for an increase in the line of credit from the
Export-Import Bank of Japan to $540 million for four
Russian projects, but the Japanese are unlikely to pro-
vide more than $400 million; the Russian side will also
be insisting on the Japanese Government allocating
funds to insure Japanese companies’ operations in the
Russian market, while the Japanese will be insisting on
the problem of the repayment of Russian debts being
cleared up.

It should be noted that the details of Soskovets’ “‘confi-
dential working meetings” with Japanese Ambassador
Watanabe in Moscow—meetings that have become a
regular event over the past few months—have been
covered in great detail by the Japanese press, and,
consequently, we know that our first vice premier is very
much counting on continuing to be the “main econo-
mist” during the visit and avoiding discussing ticklish
political topics such as, primarily, the territorial
problem.

KYODO reports that this matter was discussed at Sosk-
ovets’ latest meeting with Ambassador Watanabe
Tuesday. Also discussed were the prospects for Foreign
Minister Andrey Kozyrev’s visit to Tokyo next January,
although, insofar as we are aware, Soskovets’ job does
not include being in charge of Kozyrev. Incidentally,
there may be quite a simple explanation for this point—
KYODO notes that the first deputy premier and the
ambassador agreed that political problems would be
discussed with the Japanese by the head of the Russian
Foreign Ministry, and this obviously means that the first
vice premier will retain responsibility for economic
matters. As a theoretical plan this division of labor could
only be welcomed, were it not for the stroke of bad luck
of Japanese Deputy Foreign Minister Kunihiko Saito’s
stating that, although the Japanese side recognizes Sos-
kovets’ economic specialization, this by no means
implies that the territorial issue will be put to one side
during his visit to Japan.

One thing is reassuring and encouraging—the busy
schedule of business meetings facing the Russian delega-
tion will not be long and exhausting, since the program
has been constructed on the principle that “once the
job’s done you can let your hair down.” It became clear
today that the special aircraft carrying Soskovets and his
entourage will arrive in Tokyo around noon on Sunday,

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

14 RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

and the main documents relating to the visit will be
signed on a very tight schedule—just six hours after the
guests land, at 1710 Tokyo time the same day. Monday
will be spent on brief meetings between the delegation
head and the Japanese premier and ministers of finance,
foreign affairs, and trade and industry. On Tuesday
morning Soskovets will give a lecture at the Keidanren
(Federation of Japanese Industrialists), after lunch he
will attend a press conference on the work done in the
last day and a bit, and he will then immediately set off on
a tour of the country that will last tii December and be
pleasant in every respect.

On the eve of the delegation’s arrival there were exten-
sive rumors that the Moscow Motor Vehicle Plant
named for the Lenin Komsomol—which has been in a
difficult position for some time now—will gradually be
put back on its feet thanks to an agreement with Peugeot
of France and Toyota of Japan.

The inclusion in the Russian Government delegation of
the president of the “AZLK” Joint-Stock Company
seems to indirectly confirm these rumors and gives rise
to expectations of a major pleasant surprise. There is
only one cloud on the horizon—the thundering silence
from the Japanese side, which, as a rule, always gives its
general audience advance warning, either through hints
or minor “leaks,” about major deals that are in the
offing. So as not to languish in ignorance, the
IZVESTIYA bureau in Tokyo appealed direct to Toyota
Headquarters and, a few days later, received a written
reply from Toyota comprising two subject headings.

The first, entitled ““On the Toyota Business in Russia,”
notes that 3,800 assembled Toyotas were exported from
Japan to Russia in 1993, whereas only 1,300 had been
exported in the first nine months of this year. The most
popular Toyotas in the Russian market are the Land
Cruiser jeep and the Hiace minibus, followed closely by
the Carina and Corolla compacts. The Japanese com-
pany is devoting a great deal of attention to organizing a
network of own-brand technical service centers in Russia
and the CIS countries—there are already 12 such centers
in the biggest regional centers, including Moscow, St.
Petersburg, Khabarovsk, Vladivostok, Tashkent, and
Kiev.

The second section of Toyota’s written reply—headed
““New Directions” is emphatically laconic. It states that,
despite the rumors circulating about Toyota taking part
in the modernization of the AZLK, the corporation has
no such plans at the present time, and the question of
investing in Russian car plants does not arise.

The potential new export markets for this mighty car
corporation include only one country as an indisputable
priority—China. For China Toyota has developed a
prototype “new family car,” completed a plan for the
construction of several plants and assembly shops, and
worked out a phased timetable for investment. The
president of Toyota and all the members of the Japanese

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

supercompany’s board have already visited China sev-
eral times. None of them have as yet been to Russia, as
far as we are aware.

The second “convoy” of Japanese humanitarian aid to
the disaster-stricken Kuril Islands has left for Russia.

While it transpired for the umpteenth time that Mos-
cow’s promises to help the South Kurils, which were hit
by an earthquake, was another bluff—giving rise to
many rhetorical questions such as “‘where is the govern-
ment aid for the stricken population?” and “where is the
money?”’—Japan has been continuing to do real work.

Japanese Chief Cabinet Secretary Kojo Igarashi stated
that this time Japanese aid worth 45 million yen
($450,000) will be delivered to the islands, including
kerosene heaters and compact stoves. Moreover, human-
itarian cargo worth 82 million yen ($820,000) will be
sent to the Russian islanders through the local authori-
ties and social organizations on Hokkaido Island.

The misfortune that befell the populace of the South
Kurils has been taken very much to heart on Hokkaido—
a special “stricken islanders’ rescue fund” has even been
set up. This fund has collected more than 7 million yen
in private contributions in the past month alone.

[From “Various Aspects of Cooperation” program]

[FBIS Translated Text] As has already been reported, a
delegation of the DPRK External Economy Commission
has recently visited Russia’s Far East regions.

Observers view that the delegation’s visit was designed
to actively vitalize bilateral economic relations between
the two countries’ borderline regions.

We will hear from the Russian media on how they assess
the results of DPRK delegation’s visit to Russia’s Far
East regions: Due to the differences of opinion over the
distribution of profits between the two countries, the
DPRK-Russian agreement on jointly developing forestry
of Siberia region has been deadlocked and, thus, inflicted
heavy damage on bilateral economic relations.

In order to break the stalemated situation, a DPRK
delegation consisting of experts from various economic
fields, visited Russia’s Far East regions. Led by Han
Su-kil, vice chairman of the DPRK External Econemic
Commission, the delegation inspected the Maritime
Kray, the Amur Oblast, the Jewish Autonomous Oblast,
and the Sakhalin Oblast.

While regional reporters did not provide details on the
results of talks between the DPRK delegation and

FBIS-SOV-94-227

regional administrative officials, Han Su-kil, the head of
the delegation, assessed the visit as being very produc-
tive.

In the Sakhalin Oblast, the delegation agrecd with the
regional fishery bureau’s officials on joint efforts in
fishing. Since joint cooperation will surely bring about a
large amount of profits, this region’s administrative
officials hurriedly left for Moscow to get government
approval for additional fishing quotas.

In the Maritime Kray, the DPRK delegation inked an
agreement and a protocol of cooperation with the

slated to be opened in the seashore of Russia’s Pacific
region.

promote cooperation in the agricultural production

delegation’s visit is of significance in the fact that the
DPRK has signed an agreement based on (?foreign
currency trade), and not non- foreign currency transac-
tion trade,.

Summing up the result of his visit, the delegation head
stressed that Pyongyang will further promote amicable
relations with these regions in the future.

OPEC Hopes Russian Exports Will Be Curbed by
Severe Winter

MM2511092194 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
24 Nov 94 p 3

[Report by Boris Vinogradov: “Tropical Jakarta Hopes
for a Severe Winter in Russia’’]

{FBIS Translated Text] Whereas gasoline-starved
Moscow is looking for opportunities to increase Russia’s
oil exports, OPEC countries in Jakarta have agreed to
freeze their own production and maintain it at its current
level for the whole of 1995, putting on sale no more than
24.52 million barrels per day.

If they succeed in doing so, and none of the 12 main
exporters breaks its commitments, a marked growth of
prices is to be expected on the world oil market this
winter—possibly to $21 per barrel, against the current
price of $17 per barrel. Such is the conclusion reached by
experts analyzing OPEC’s decision.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 15

At the same time it is noted that current trends are
moving in favor of Russia, which remains the third
largest oil-producer after Saudi Arabia and the United
States. This, despite the fact that overall production und
consumptior within the country is in free fall. Thus
while Russia produced 354 million tonnes of oil in 1993,
it produced only 310 million tonnes in 1994.

However, the meagerness of our own oil share is not
stopping Russians from increasing exports. Whiie in
1993 the “Transneft” company overtook the maximum
level reached by the former Unior by 82.7 million
tonnes, this year it has increased that figure to 84 million
tonnes. The rate of growth is to some decree secured at
the expense of our Commonwealth partners. In the
current year CIS countries have received from Russia
not 48 million tonnes, as was the case last year, but only
30 million.

At a recent conference Vice Premier Oleg Soskovets
demanded that several more Russian companies be
given access to the export oil pipelines. No one knows
what limits exports could reach.

In this situation OPEC countries are warily watching to
see that Russia, which possesses the potential to create
panic and chaos on the oil exchanges, does not upset
their plans. At any rate, at the demand of Libya and
Qatar the conference in Jakarta provided for the option
of a six-month freeze if Russia and the North Sea
oil-producing countries take no active measures to cap-
ture the market.

True, some participants in the Jakarta meeting hope that
a severe winter and the threat of a freeze in the northern
territories will prevent Russia’s “oi! kings” from doing
so. In this they rely on weather forecasts predicting
severe cold spells in regions near the Arctic Circle. One
participant in the conference said that only a cold winter
will restrain the Russians and allow us [OPEC] to obtain
the prices which existed at the height of the 1990 crisis in
the Persian Gulf.

Pacific Nuclear Pollution Conference Planned

MM2411122394 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
24 Nov 94 p 4

[Report by Natalya Ostrovskaya: “Submerged Subma-
rine Reactors Sena Out ‘SOS””]

{[FBIS Translated Text] Vladivostok—Thanks to the
notorious liquid radioactive waste and its discharge into
the Sea of Japan what specialists consider to be the far
more real problem of solid radioactive waste contami-
nation in the northern Pacific Ocean and the three Far
East seas has remained in the background.

This waste is made up of the products obtained from
recycling [utilizatsiya] nuclear submarines plus the waste
products from Japanese and U.S. nuclear power stations
which are buried at sea.

A letter from U.S. scientists at the Center for Research
into the Problem of Nuclear Waste (Mississippi) to the
Far East Branch of the International Green Cross envi-
ronmental organization in Vladivostok provides con-
vincing evidence that there is a problem. It says that the
Japanese-Russian-U.S. group set up to resolve the
problem of dumping nuclear waste in the Seas of Japan
and Okhotsk and the Arctic Ocean will meet 12-13
January next year at the Gulf Coast Hotel, Mississippi.
Representatives of the Far East’s Green Cross who have
information regarding the problem of the contamination
of the Sea of Japan are invited to attend.

Evidently the people who wrote the letter to Vladivostok
are interested in more detailed, thorough information
than that which they have at the moment. Thanks to the
efforts of one member of the international group of
experts, Professor Aleksey Yablokov, chairman of the
Russian Federation Interdepartmental Commission for
Environmental Safety, figures that, for all that they are
general, are truly shocking are known. Thus, the message
to the people of Vladivostok says that during the “Cold
War” the Russians discharged 2.5 million curies of
radioactive waste, twice the official figure. Some of this
““dose”’ is to be found in the 18 nuclear reactors that were
dumped in the sea along with their fuel. The already
complex environmental situation is aggravated by the
Pacific currents, thanks to which, the scientists write, the
nuclear waste dumped by the Russians “floats” toward
Alaska.

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

Green Cross Regional Coordinator Aleksandr Malyshev
regards the involvement of the people of the Far East in
the meeting on the problems of nuclear wasie in Missis-
Sippi as one step toward the implementation of this
project. Aleksandr Malyshev said that oceanologists,
ecologists, and representatives of the Pacific Fleet will
leave for the United States.

U.S. Interest in Russian Rockets, Launching
Complexes Noted

LD2411111694 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 0741
GMT 24 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Aleksandr Pakhomov]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] New York November 24
TASS—The United States plans to modernise its obso-
lete carrier-rocket launchers with the help of Russian
experts. The Americans are displaying particular interest
in the automated launching complexes, developed by the
Moscow transport machine-building designing bureau
for “Zenit” and “Tsiklon” carrier-rockets.

A representative of the U.S. Air Force recently attended
the launching of a “Zenit” rocket from the Baykonur
Cosmodrome to check the effectiveness of these com-
plexes. “‘We were amazed by the simplicity and effi-
ciency of preparations to launch the Zenit’, an unnamed
high-ranking representative of the U.S. Air Force told
the magazine “AVIATION WEEK AND SPACE TECH-
NOLOGY”. “Nobody had to be on the launching plat-
form, because everything was done automatically,
including the placing of the carrier-rocket in the vertical
position, its connection to the supply systems, and the
launching proper.”

Specialists of the Russian designing bureau are currently
visiting the Cape Canaveral cosmodrome in Florida at
the invitation of the ““Martin-Marietta” corporation to
check the possibility of modernising “Atlas” launching
complexes and eventually the launching facilities for the
most powerful American “Titan” rocket. “Too much
time is being spent in the United States to prepare the
launching, and we hope to cut it down by means of
Russian technologies,” Robert Ford, one of the man-
agers of the ““Martin-Marietta” corporation, noted. The
corporation’s engineers, as well as representatives of the
U.S. Air Force, were staggered by the fact that the
“Zenit” launching complex took only five hours to get
ready for the next start.

In addition to launching complexes, the “‘Martin-
Marietta” is also interested in Russian rocket engines. It
plans to install them on modernised “Atlas” rockets. The
idea of manufacturing such engines in the United States
is now being considered. The corporation recently pur-
chased in Russia several hundred litres of kerosene,
which proved to be better than the American as regards
density and other characteristics. The ‘*Martin-
Marietta” plans to put it to several tests with a view to
using this kerosene in “Atlas” launching engines“.

Ecological ‘Cedar’ Movement Becomes Party

MK2411121094 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
24 Nov 94 p 3

[Report by Irina Vladykina from Yekaterinburg under
the “Organizational Party Development” rubric:
““Cedar’ Is Making Second Attempt”’]

{[FBIS Translated Text] The “Cedar” constructive-
ecological movement, which came into being before the
elections for the Federal Assembly late in 1993, has
turned into an ecological “Cedar” party. The founding
congress of this party was held in Yekaterinburg with
messages of greetings coming from Arkadiy Volskiy,
Ivan Silayev, and leaders of the Federation of Indepen-
dent Trade Unions of Russia.

Anatoliy Panfilov, who heads the party, believes that in
addition to the educational work with directors of enter-
prises and the population, it will be able to deal directly
with nature conservation agencies and ecological inspec-
tion services.

Last year’s failure (the movement fell short by 5 percent
of the required votes, for which reason it is not repre-
sented in the State Duma) does not deter the organizers
of the party. They intend to campaign against practices
where finances come from separate ecological public
organizations from Russia and other countries, and to
establish a unified independent ecological fund.

Academician Aleksey Yablokov, a leader of the new
party, stated at the congress that the ‘““Green”’ movement
today has two strategies: one is a sharp politicization,
and the other is avoiding all politics, which may result in
a split. Aleksey Yablokov told your SEGODNYA corre-
spondent that for more than a decade “‘Cedar”’ has been
drawing a skeptical attitude from many public organiza-
tions devoted to environmental protection, both because
they lay claim to being the leader in the ecology move-
ment, and because of the new party’s undisguised con-
nection with state-run and commercial structures.
