saries would never see him doing either of the two things.

The powers of Tajuddin were confirmed by the depart-
ment’s presidium, muftis of the Middle Volga Region,
Tatarstan, the Rostov, St.Petersburg, Sverdlovsk and
other Russian regions who were fresent at the press
conference.

Tajuddin said the split in the Central Spiritual Depart-
ment of Moslems of Russia and the CIS European
Countries was a result of activities of foreign Moslem
organizations and fundamentalist foundations based in
Arab countries. He warned against fanning discord
between Russian Moslems of different ethnic belonging.
He said if Islam was opposed to other confessions this
could trigger off a conflict similar to that in ex-
Yugoslavia.

Tajuddin blamed the Bashkir authorities for interfering
into affairs of the religious community. He said tnat in
particular the Council on Religions under the Bashkir
government had approved the decisions of the congress.
He said that as a result the bank accounts of the Central
Spiritual Department of Moslems had been frozen and
could be transferred to pretenders. He said he expected
the office of the Central Spiritual Department of Mos-
lems in Ufa to be captured.

Tajuddin, a Tatar, 46 years old, was elected the
Chairman of the Central Spiritual Department of Mos-
lems of Russia and the CIS European Countries in 1992.

DPR Demands Report on Social Accord Treaty

MM2511130994 Moscow ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI in
Russian 23 Nov 94 p 2

[Commentary by Lyubov Tsukanova: “The Treaty Is For
the Conciliation of Interests, Not for the Purpose of
Blackmail’’]

[FBIS ranslated Text] The Democratic Party of Russia
[DPR] National Council and the DPR faction in the
State Duma have demanded the immediate convocation
of the Civil Accord Assembly in order for the federal
organs of power to report on the fulfillment of the
commitments which they undertook in the Treaty on
Social Accord. In the opinion of the faction’s leaders, the
government is not keeping its promises, the socioeco-
nomic situation in the country is deteriorating, and
hence at its next congress the party could raise the
question of removing its signature from the treaty.

For all the scepticism of a significant part of society
toward the treaty, this statement attests at least to the
fact that the Treaty on Social Accord has become a real

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

factor in political life which is recognized and appealed
to, not only by its initiators, but also by the moderate
opposition. The treaty has been in force for more than
six months, and in this time, Arkadiy Voiskiy, leader of
the Russian Union of Industrialists and Entrepreneurs
{[RUIE], said at a press conference last Friday [19
November], “We cannot fail to note that there has been
something of a lull.”” The RUIE leader is not convinced
that this “lull” will last long, but that the country got
through the summer and fall relatively calmly is a fact
which cannot be denied. While not considering the treaty
to be a panacea against all political storms, Arkadiy
Volskiy is nevertheless convinced that work to create
conciliation mechanisms and procedures must be con-
tinued.

But there is another fact which it is impossible to deny:
There has been no let-up in attempts to undermine the
process of conciliation in society. The DPR is by no
means the first “signatory” to use its signature to the
treaty to put pressure on the authorities. Mark Masar-
skiy, one of the leaders of the ‘“roundtable’—the Rus-
sian Federation President's Public Chamber—and a
member of the conciliation commission, believes that
“intimidation is an improper method,” especially since
it is ineffective: Retracting their signature does not mean
that, after seceding from the treaty, the “‘apostates” will
employ different methods. However, it is necessary to
acknowledge another fact also: Attacks on the treaty by
its participants objectively reinforce the prestige of the
irreconcilable opposition, which does not recognize the
conciliation process and has been recently stepping up its
statements on the need to change the “regime.”

Arkadiy Volskiy is right in saying that accords make
sense only if they are fulfilled. Those who say that, fully
or partially, the government has not fulfilled the
majority of its commitments under the treaty are, alas,
also right. But it is also a fact that the agreement is being
badly fulfilled by all sides—by parliament, which has
still not adopted a number of fundamental acts of the
first importance; by entrepreneurs, who often, in Mark
Kasarskiy’s words, behave “antinationally”; and by
political chiefs and public organization leaders, who
have “lobbed” into the public arena the ideas of early
elections, or, on the contrary, of extending the term of
the existing organs of power. When not only the Com-
munists, but also “Yabloko” leader Grigoriy Yavlinskiy
talk about early presidential elections, this means that
ideas of a rule-of-law state are still just so much hot air,
not only for those who have never accepted them, but
also for some democrats. It is one thing when the
Communists ignore the Constitution: The statement by
the Communist Party of the Russian Federation pub-
lished recently in PRAVDA and SOVETSKAYA
ROSSIYA is only a continuation of their policy. It is
another thing when leaders pretending to independence
and parading their adherence to democratic principles
begin to play someone else's tune. At any rate, this has
already given Gennadiy Zyuganov the right to assume
that leaders of the other democratic organizations will

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

follow Yavlinskiy in demanding early elections. It gives
Petr Romanov, member of the Federation Council—a
“leading light of the opposition”—the right to state
already today that he is ready to take on responsibility
for the country, either as president or as premier.

There is nothing terrible in the fact that political forces
are seeking power—that is normal for a democratic
country. What is bad is that no one wants to be patient
and carry his burden for even the short period which is
defined in the Constitution as transitional. The Treaty
on Social Accord was concluded precisely so that the
country should be saved from upheavals during this
period and so that the new Constitution should come
into force in its entirety without let or hindrance. The
impatience of politicians has frequently played a fateful
role in Russia’s history. It is more difficult to negotiate
than to issue ultimatums or go for broke. But it is more
productive. It is for this purpose, and not for that of
self-assertion, that the democratic methods of concili-
ating interests provided by the Treaty on Social Accord
should be used.

Fedorov Urges Democrats To Quit Government

MK2411092094 Moscow KURANTY in Russian
24 Nov 94 p 1

[Statement by Boris Fedorov under the “Opinion”
rubric: “Boris Fedorov: ’The Authorities Have Turned
Their Backs on Democrats and Reforms]

[FBIS Translated Text] Yesterday, Boris Fedorov, State
Duma deputy and Liberal Democratic Union chairman,
made the following statement to a KURANTY corre-
spondent.

Tension is increasing in the mass media in recent days in
regard to the upcoming voting in the State Duma on the
1995 budget. In the process, deputies are being intimi-
dated by a possible disbandment of the State Duma
resulting from its failure to pass the budget, and parlia-
mentary elections are predicted to take place in spring
1995.

I categorically reject pushing through the State Duma
any decisions using blackmail. Such actions have nothing
to do with democracy and are even more dangerous than
a bad budget. Nobody is going to intimidate us. Only
politically bankrupt people who are afraid of looking
their voters in the eye can be scared of elections.

I disagree that the nonacceptance of the budget should
necessarily entail a dissolution of the State Duma. What
is preventing the president from replacing the government,
drawing up a new budget, starting genuine reforms?
Finally, | doubt that the president, with his services in
fighting communism, will decide to dissolve what is
actually the third parliament in four years.

I call on all freedom-loving citizens to give a rebuff to the
attempts to view the situation in the country exclusively
in black and white. Most people in Russia have long been

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 19

using color televisions, and they can see that four polit-
ical centers have been formed—the essentially nonparty
colorless authority, communists, Zhirinovskiyites, and a
democratic opposition to the first three groups.

The opposition of democrats is taking shape not because
some people are joining hands with the communists, but
because the authorities have turned their backs on dem-
ocrats and reforms. The remaining democrats should quit
the government, which is incapable of solving society’s
vital problems.

FCS Edict Grants Investigation, Security Power

MK2411083094 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
24 Nov 94 p 1

[Report by Aleksandr Barinov, Aleksandr Kudakayev in
the “State Security” column: “What Did We Fight For?
The President Has Restored the FCS Investigation Func-
tions”]

{[FBIS Translated Text] Yesterday President Yeltsin
signed the edict “On Creating an Investigation Direc-
torate of the Federal Counterintelligence Service of the
Russian Federation [FCS] and Investigation Units in
Counterintelligence Bodies of the Russian Federation.”
Under the document, the new—or rather restored—
service is entrusted to organize and conduct preliminary
investigation on matters referring to FCS jurisdiction.
To organize an investigation apparatus, the authorized
strength of the counterintelligence service (including
military and civilian personnel) is to be increased by
1,000. Sources say the creation of an investigation appa-
ratus in the FCS structure also envisions the return to
this department of Lefortovo prison. Therefore Russian
state security has fully restored its powers that existed
before 21 December 1993.

FCS representatives, predictably, met the presidential
edict with approval. Aleksandr Zdanovich, deputy chief
of the FCS Public Relations Center, told SEGODNYA’s
correspondent that the restoration of investigation bod-
ies—in his opinion, unreasonably abolished in
December 1993 in the process of reorganizing the Secu-
rity Ministry as the FCS—directly responds to state
interests in intensifying the struggle against especially
dangerous crimes.

It should be noted that representatives of the Ministry of

Internal Affairs and the Prosecutor’s Office have repeat-
edly noted that the investigation of criminal cases, which

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 Nevember 1994

earlier referred to the jurisdiction of the Security Min-
istry (espionage, smuggling, and so forth), proved to be a
fairly complicated task for the latter owing to the inves-
tigators’ overload and their insufficient qualifications in
this highly specific sphere.

To sum up, Yeltsin's edict last year on dissolving the
Security Ministry, caused by the president’s opinion that
“*... the state security system of the Russian Federation
has outlived itself, is ineffective and burdensome on the
state budget, and hinders political and economic
reform,” has proved to be not effective enough.

Justice Minister Yuriy Kalmykov believes that the need
to step up the fight against terrorism makes the FCS’
investigative functions quite legitimate. At the same
time, Kalmykov does not rule out that in the future it
would be logical to merge all investigation structures
under a single leadership.

have been interrogated in this case, among them repre-
sentatives of the “power structures.”” And Nikolay Kov-
alev, deputy director of the Federal Counterintelligence

the clock.

CIS Experts Analyze Air Accident Causes

MM2411100194 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
23 Nov 94 p 15

[Report by Viktor Belikov: “CIS Air Experts: Given
Current Discipline and Practices in Civil Aviation the
Number of Crashes Will Increase. Interstate Aviation
Committee Analysis of the Causes of the Unprecedented
Accident Rate on Our Airlines”’}

[FBIS Translated Text] Some representatives of a very
unusual profession—60 top-class air accident investiga-
tors from the CIS countries—met last week at the
Interstate Aviation Committee. Each of the seminar
participants had a wealth of experience of work as pilot,
flight engineer, or air traffic controller. Their collective
assessment of the current accident situation on the
Commonwealth countries’ airlines is not just alarming—
it will dismay anyone who has been a passenger on an
aircraft even once.

Although 1994 is not yet over it is clear already that it
will be the worst in the history of civil aviation in the CIS
(and the former USSR). Between the beginning of the
year and the middle of November there were 57 recorded
air incidents, including 18 crashes which cost the lives of
318 people. Compare this with the whole of 1993, when
there were 106 incidents, including 14 crashes resulting
in 197 fatalities.

The professionals were bound to be worried by the
comparison provided by these grim statistics on the
number of aircraft written off, that is, broken up: Last
year it was 48, this year only 35 aircraft and helicopters.
The one-fourth reduction in the number of aircraft
destroyed given an overall increase in the number of
fatalities leads to the depressing conclusion that more
so-called heavy passenger aircraft on regular services are
crashing.

The experts stated that regular services (scheduled
flights) accounted for eight incidents, but three crashes
led to the deaths of 228 passengers and crew. In 1993
there were two crashes and 194 fatalities. The worst
offenders are Tu-154, Tu-134, An-24, and Yak-40 air-
craft.

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

The report by flight safety commission chairman R.
Teymurazov and the contributions from Interstate Avi-
ation Committee seminar participants contained a
detailed analysis of the obvious decline in the standard
of reliability of our aircraft as mass passenger transport.

The chief cause is the human factor, in other words,
gross violations by air traffic controllers and flight crew
of the rules governing the safe operation of aircraft. A
fateful, one might say, sinister role is played by ill-
conceived “market relations,” which are eating away at
the old established air transport system like rust.

There is frequent mention, for example, of overloaded
aircraft or disregard for the center of gravity in order to
make extra profit every trip. Crews paying cash for fuel
at “foreign” airports are taking on the minimum
amount—again out of “economic expediency.” As a
result they sometimes lack the reserves of kerosene to be
able to go on to an emergency airfield if necessary. They
have to land even if weather conditions have deterio-
rated drastically, which is bound to lead to accidents,
particularly when the pilots often are not properly
trained to make such difficult landings.

As for crew training, it was noted at the seminar that one
of the most likely causes of the crash of the A-310 Airbus
near Mezhdurechensk was the Russian pilots’ unfamil-
iarity with the controls of a West European-made air-
craft. The problem becomes particularly acute and sig-
nificant in connection with the influx of foreign aircraft
into CIS countries’ airlines.

Incidentally, it turns out that the black box recordings of
the flight parameters of Boeings and Airbuses cannot be
decoded by Russian equipment, which has quite dif-
ferent specifications. So trips are made without objective
monitoring facilities to analyze the progress of the flight.
It is getting to the point where accident situations have to
be roughly assessed later. Economies by aircraft factories
do not allow for expensive decoding of black box record-
ings.

The purely practical, specialist seminar was not intended
to elaborate any general recommendations on flight
safety “rules.” Particularly as these rules were estab-
lished long ago and are incorporated in flight operation
manuals and instructions. They need only be strictly
adhered to everywhere and in all things if we want to
reverse the very dangerous trend of increasing accidents
involving aircraft. As current practice shows, this is the
most difficult professional duty of aviators.

Ministry on Lower Food Production, Higher
Imports

LD2411205794 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1923
GMT 24 Nov 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Russia’s Agriculture and Food
Ministry puts this year’s total domestic output of meat

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

and meat products at 3.1 million tonnes, that of whole
milk products at 6.4 million tonnes, that of butter at
475,000 tonnes and that of vegetable oil at 780,000
tonnes, which respectively accounts for 75, 82, 68 and 78
percent of last year’s amounts.

The state of sugar production is “particularly alarming,”
a ministry official told Interfax: this year’s output of it is
not expected to exceed 2.6 million tonnes, which is 67
percent what was produced in 1993. What explains this
is a poorer sugar bect harvest, which in 1994 is unlikely
to top 15 million tonnes, compared to last year’s 25.5
million. That, in turn, has been caused by cuts in total
area under the crop (trimmed to 222,000 hectares - 17
percent down on last year) and lower yield, which went
down from 2.7 tonnes per hectare in 1993 to 1.9 tonnes
this year.

In the meantime, Russia has kept increasing its imports
of some food items, experts say. In the 10 months from
January to October, the country imported 325,000
tonnes of frozen meat, 314,000 tonnes of poultry and
154,000 tonnes of butter, which is respectively 390, 500,
and 200 percent up on the same period last year.

However, in the same 10 months Russia bought abroad
only 2.7 million tonnes of grain, $12,000 tonnes of raw
sugar and 928,000 tonnes of refined sugar - 71, 49, and
17 percent down on the same period in 1993.

Moscow Looks To Change Oil Balance Guidelines
LD2411095594 Moscow INTERFAX in English 0625
GMT 24 Nov 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Russia proposes to change the
guidelines for making up the balances of oil and oil

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 23

products if the fuel export quotas are lifted, a source in
the Russian government told the Petroleum Information

Agency.

He said that the payable demand of Russia’s regions,
federal needs and the amounts to be exported would be
recognized in making up the balances.

Under the existing regulations on distribution of oil and
refinery products, the making up of balances is the
responsibility of the Economy and Fuel and Energy
Ministries which determine the amounts to be consumed
inside the country and exported. The annual and addi-
tional export quotas are assigned to businesses by the
Russian government’s daily management commission.

When this practice is discontinued, the source believes,
the oil companies will have to work out balances of oil
and product production and consumption which will
reflect the agreements signed with the country’s regions
and the potential of free sales inside and outside Russia.
The companies will be required to keep the consumers
supplied.

The Fuel and Energy Ministry is expected to accumulate
the data from the oil companies in its own balance which
would allow for the export for the state needs, the
amount of which will be determined by the Economy
Ministry.

The Petroleum Information Agency found that the state-
owned Rosneft company which runs non-privatized oil
installations in Russia had forecast a 1995 balance. It
plans to extract 121,726,000 tonnes of oil in 1995 or
which 27,324,000 will be exported.

Parliament on Credits To West, 1995 Budget

MK2311144094 Moscow KOMMERSANT-DAILY in
Russian 23 Nov 94 p 3

[Report by Andrey Bagrov: “The Hearings Took Place
Behind Closed Doors”]

{[FBIS Translated Text] For the first time since the
previous Supreme Soviet, the Russian parliament has
returned to the practice of closed hearings on economic
matters. Yesterday, behind closed doors, the State Duma
discussed the Russian foreign debt problem. The parlia-
mentarians were so inhospitable primarily because the
ultimate formulation of the federal 1995 budget today
depends on the foreign debt situation.

In spite of the express mysteriousness of the hearings,
their progress and outcome could essentially be pre-
dicted beforehand. Obviously, the participants spoke
with special interest about using new Western credits to
cover part of next year’s budget deficit: This original idea
became a component part of the 1995 federal budget
concept. Of the planned 72 trillion rubles budget deficit,
30.6 trillion (or 42.5 percent) is proposed to be covered

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

eo *

from outside financial sources. Predictably, the parlia-
mentarians expressed their bewilderment over the gov-
ernment’s confidence that new Western credits for
closing the budget deficit will indeed be received, espe-
cially considering the present foreign debt situation. No
official explanations were made on this count, however:
Vice Premier Oleg Davydov, overseeing this problem, is
accompanying Viktor Chernomyrdin on a tour of Per-
sian Gulf states, while Aleksandr Shokhin, who was
present at the hearings and who as former vice premier
was Russia’s chief foreign debt negotiator, can now be
regarded as a mouthpiece of the official government
position on the issue only with a measure of irony.

Meanwhile the current foreign debt situation is quite
noteworthy. According to unofficial information
announced at the hearings, the aggregate sum of Russian
debts has reached $112.7 billion. According to official
data, the overall debt of the former USSR is $80 billion
(Russia’s main partner at the debt rescheduling negotia-
tions is the Paris Ciub of creditor states, which Russia
owes $50 billion; the second largest creditor is the
London club of private banks: $24 billion; and finally,
the Russian Government owes $6 billion to private
firms). Citing government sources, however, the figure of
$103.9 billion was mentioned as foreign debts inherited
by Russia from the USSR. For its part Russia is owed
approximately $160 billion. Oleg Davydov proposes that
debts be primarily paid to private firms while debts to
the Paris and London clubs be postponed to a later date.
Such an approach, rather than reducing debt service
payments, can lead to a situation wherein Russia will
have to pay debts on the least favorable terms because
the hopes of getting out of making substantial payments
to official creditors are not destined to materialize.

Even so, the experience of Russia’s interrelations with its
creditors shows that even the most irrational policy
followed by Russian authorities in the foreign debt
sphere is not necessarily an obstacle to getting new
credits: And in this respect t's government’s budget
plans are not doomed to failure. Actually the govern-
ment is offering the West a deal: credits in exchange for
at least a semblance of budgetary stabilization. Quite
possibly, the West will be ready to accept it.

Official on Foreign Debt, Former USSR Credits

LD2311144794 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1123
GMT 23 Nov 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] As of January |, 1994 Russia’s
aggregate debt to foreign creditors was $112.7 billion,
deputy head of the currency-credit department under
Russia’s Foreign Economic Relations Ministry, Yuriy
Gromushkin, reported to Interfax on Wednesday.

He explained that this debt is the result of credits
received by Russia from international organizations and
foreign governments beginning with 1992—totaling $8.8
billion, and of credits granted to the former USSR
amounting to $103.9 billion. Russia owes over $80

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

billion of this sum to Western countries, members of the
Paris and London clubs, about $20 billion—to all its
other creditors, including South Korea, Persian Gulf
countries, among others.

Being the legal successor of the former USSR, the
Russian Federation took the obligation to service the
USSR’s entire debt, including the share of the former
Soviet republics which have transferred their part of
former USSR assets to Russia.

Forecasts of the Russian foreign debt rising to $130
billion by the end of 1995 are connected with prelimi-
nary assessments of the size of Russia’s likely new
borrowings in the immediate future.

Ministries Vie To Settle Oil Export Questions

MM72511135394 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
24 Nov 94 p 1

[Report by Mikhail Berger: ““The Question of Freedom
for Our Exports Could Cost at Least $7 Billion’)

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

[FBIS Translated Text] In the upper echelons recently
there have been particularly heated and even fierce
discussions over the problem of export quotas for oil and
petroleum products. Statements on the continuation of
market reforms presuppose the further liberalization of
foreign trade, including the trade in energy sources. The
situation regarding the supply of fuel to domestic con-
sumers (the gasoline crisis in Moscow and the frozen
northern territories), however, provides ammunition for
the opponents of unregulated oil exports.

By an irony of fate the problem of export quotas and
licenses for energy sources is “celebrating” its third
anniversary now. Many people have probably already
forgotten that it was in November 1991 that the first set
of presidential edicts, prepared by Yegor Gaydar's team,
appeared. The edict on the liberalization of foreign trade
was a crucial one.

Freedom for exporters was supposed to begin on 1 July
1992, then on | January 1993, then on the next “‘eco-
nomic Monday”—1 January 1994.

The latest “‘solemn” promise to abolish all export quotas
and licenses was formulated in the president's special
edict of 23 May this year in his well-known package of
spring economic edicts. Skeptical liberal observers stated
at that time that they were shocked by the revolutionary
nature of this edict but they asked for a delay until | July
1994 when the promised freedom was supposed to begin.
July 1 came but freedom didn’t. Instead what happened
on that day was that the president issued a new edict on
this theme interpreting the previous revolutionary paper
to mean that | January 1995 would mark the beginning
of an even tougher and more comprehensive regulation
of oil exports.

Over the past three years the keen debate between the
liberals and the “practitioners” in the government over
whether or not to supply Russian energy sources to the
free market has first erupted, then died down. The
liberals consider this necessary. The “practitioners”
claim that export freedom will hit Russian domestic
consumers (the echoes of this debate can be “heard” in
the reportage by our correspondent in icy Arkhangelsk
published in today’s issue of IZVESTIYA).

There are at least two reasons for the latest eruption of
the government debate on oil exports. First, the position
of the liberals has strengthened considerably in the
government; the second (and perhaps the “main’’)
reason is that Russia is hoping to receive big money—
around $7.5 billion—from the international financial
organizations in the very near future.

The 1995 budget policy aiso hinges on that money. It
should be said that Russia is close to receiving those
credits. The sole but absolutely insuperable obstacle is
the retention of the oil and petroleum products export
quota system.

The signal that Russia might not get the promised
billions was given by the IMF decision to withhold its

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 25

promised credit to another CIS state—Belorussia
[Belarus]. INTERFAX has reported that the IMF has
suspended discussion of the allocation of a $308 million
credit to Belorussia. The reason is the Belorussian pres-
ident’s edict on restoring prices to the levels of early
November.

So the passion for state regulation could cost Russia very
dear as well. First Vice Premier Anatoliy Chubays and
Economics Minister Yevgeniy Yasin, addressing Tues-
day’s session of the KOV [expansion unknown],
demanded very firmly that the quota system be abol-
ished. Furthermore, the Economics Ministry's forecasts
show that even with complete export liberalization there
will not be a price explosion.

However, the Ministry of Foreign Economic Relations
insists On retaining oil export regulation. But what about
the credits? The Ministry of Foreign Economic Relations
has officially submitted proposals to the government
which would formally scrap export quotas but in fact
retain full state contrul over them. The foreign economic
department is proposing to introduce as of | January
1995 a balance for the consumption of oil and petroleum
products in the domestic market. Thus these are not
export quotas, it seems, but a purely internal matter. In
practice what this will mean is that the government is
ratifying a domestic oil consumption balance which in
principle is hardly different from internal licensing. In
other words, the oil that is produced will be distributed
by the state to domestic consumers.

But in order to export oil you first have to produce it in
Russia and here the Ministry of Foreign Economic
Relations is already lying in wait to impose its conditions
on potential exporters.

Oleg Davydov, vice premier for foreign economic policy
and minister for foreign economic relations, added a
memorandum to those proposals in which he said that
almost all the economic departments are in agreement
except the Economic Ministry. It remains to be seen
whose arguments prove stronger: those of the Ministry of
Foreign Economic Relations or those of the Economics
Ministry?

Yasin Announces End of Oil Price Regulation

LD2411203194 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1923
GMT 24 Nov 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] On January |, 1995 the Russian
Government intends to fully give up the regulation of the
prices of oil and oil products in the domestic market. In
practice this means that the last administrative barrier
will be removed that sets a 50 percent ceiling on the
profitability of oil extracting companies.

This was disclosed Thursday in an exclusive interview
with the Financial Information Agency by the Eco-
nomics Minister Yevgeniy Yasin.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

26 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

He said the step is largely formal because for objective
reasons the profitability of the oil industry has not
exceeded 20-30 percent for a long time.

“If the figure grows, the 37 trillion of debts in the
industry will increase by at least a half, and the drop in
demand will hit oil extraction hard”, the minister said.

Yasin described as a more radical and effective step the
planned abolition of oil and oil product export quotas as
well as the possible elimination of the group of special
exporters. If the procedure is radically revised, the ques-
tion of oil exports will be decided solely by producers
and customers which will change the balance of
resources directed to the international and domestic
markets, Yasin said.

Still he does not think that the abolition of quotas and
deregulation of exports can lead to the uncontrolled
exportation of oil to the detriment of Russian interests.
The minister suggested introducing export duties that
would even out oil prices in the domestic and interna-
tional markets. In this case the incentive will disappear
for large-scale exports forming the foundation for satu-
rating the Russian market with oil products.

However, the minister refused to name concrete changes
in the size of export duties saying it is necessary to
discuss his proposals with the cabinet (most experts
predict that customs duties will drop from 30 to 22-25
ECUs for a ton of oil).

Yasin thinks the prices of oil and oil products will
stabilize in Russia quite fast but at a higher level. Oil
prices are expected to grow 100 percent.

Independent experts believe this will be caused by a
growing demand for fuel and energy in the country
following the deregulation of exports rather than infla-
tion and growing extraction costs.

establishments, institutions, organizations, and enter-

Commission Works To Improve Military
Payments

MK2411093094 Moscow KOMMERSANT-DAILY in
Russian 24 Nov 34 p 3

[Report by Vladislav Borodulin: ““The Government Has
Promised To Foot 70 Percent of the Military Bill”)

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

[FBIS Translated Text] Perhaps for the first time in its
existence, the government commission on improving the
payments and settlements system yesterday discussed
the problem of arrears in a clearly nonproduction sphere:
the army. At first sight the resolution of this problem lies
on the political plane: to pay or not to pay from the
deficit-ridden budget. Today, however, it is especially
important for the authorities to demunstrate their atten-
tion to army problems: The upcoming discussion of the
1995 budget in parliament could become fatal for the
Chernomyrdin cabinet, and the attempt at least some-
what to neutralize the opponents appears quite appro-
priate.

The commission leadership decided that the discussion
of the problem of nonpayment by the state to its army
should be closed to the press. Mass media representa-
tives were allowed only to the so- called “parquet”—the
first several minutes of the sessions. After that, Oleg
Soskovets, citing “‘an abundance of confidential infor-
mation,” left commission members one to one with
Defense Ministry representatives.“

The gist of the statements by the military was that
according to their estimates, 87.8 trillion rubles was
required this year for a “normal” functioning of the
army. A compromise figure—S55 trillion—could also suit
the ministry, but this year’s budget provides for military
spending of 40.6 trillion. The military's irritation is
aggravated by the fact that even the money promised by
the government is not being fully released by the Finance
Ministry. According to various estimates, the debt
ranges from | trillion to 5 trillion rubles. Such substan-
tial discrepancies are due to the fact that the Defense
Ministry includes into state arrears also the indebtedness
on state order payments.

Yesterday, however, there was no discussion as such:
Settlements with the army must be made in full, above
all on salaries to officers and enlisted men—this was the
thrust of statements by commission members. To be
sure, traditional qualifications were made to the effect
that priorities need to be defined while funds must be
dissipated. In the end, on Oleg Soskovets’ insistence, it
was decided that the government must undertake to
finance 70 percent of the military spending envisioned in
this year’s budget. Anatoliy Chubays is skeptical of the
feasibility of such pledges. According to him, the govern-
ment was originally planning to ensure a 66- percent
execution but in fact it will be between 62 and 64
percent. In this situation, however, the specific figures of
military budget execution are not so important after all.
The government acutely needs allies in debates on the
1995 budget. In this respect yesterday’s session was
called upon somewhat to mitigate the negative effects of
the sharp statements made by the country’s political
leadership this year: the president’s, on the need drasti-
cally to downsize the army; and the premier’s, that
Russia does not need that many howitzers.

Filatov Recommends ‘Compatible’ Team Members

LD2311174394 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1602
GMT 23 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Tamara Zamyatina]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow November 23 TASS—
Head of the newly-created Presidential Information
Department and presidential spokesman have to be
“compatible” in order to work as one team, chief of
Russian President Boris Yeltsin’s Administration Sergey
Filatov said.

Filatov has recommended the president to appoint
Sergey Nosovets, 36, as head of his Information Depart-
ment. However, Yeltsin has apparently decided to wait
untii his aides find a replacement for his outgoing
spokesman Vyacheslav Kostikov.

The president believes that the head of the Information
Department and a new spokesman have to be psycho-
logically compatible to act as one team. In addition, the
spokesman has the rank of presidential aide and his
status is higher than that of the Information Department
chief, and therefore the latter’s candidacy has to suit the
former.

Filatov told ITAR-TASS that he will oversee the presi-
dential Information Department, while the new
spokesman will be subordinate to Yeltsin’s right-hand
man Viktor Ilyushin, who heads the team of presidential
aides.

Filatov said he and Ilyushin have no disagreements over
the plan of the reorganisation of the presidential admin-
istration, which will in particular abolish duplicating
offices, including the Department of Information Sup-
port, the operative information centre and the press
service. They will be transformed into one single infor-
mation department.

expressing disagreement with the announced intentions.

“... On the economic side, this merger will not help
seriously to cut government expenditure on television
and radio. It is well known that payments for the
Communications Ministry’s services account for the
lion’s share of this expenditure. Only 15 percent of the
television and radio company’s budget is being spent on
making programs and maintaining its personnel. For this
reason we think it essential immediately to start studying
terms and conditions of converting one of the channels
into a joint-stock company. Meanwhile, the other
channel, upon acquiring the status of state channel,
could be financed from the state budget.

... On the political side, the merger of the television and
radio companies into one group would cause the support
base for democratic transformations and economic
reform to shrink... Journalists and technicians of the
ARSTRC, many of whom left the USSR State Television

collective...

with two different types of organization: one of them was
registered as a mass media company (“Ostankino”),
while the other is a state enterprise (ARSTRC). It should
be recalled that last year, thanks to your support, our
company was transformed into a state enterprise.

politics from which it (the conscience) is trying to keep as
far away from as possible. Will the sole practical result of
the Yeltsin-intelligentsia meeting be the breakup of the
ARSTRC, which in the view of certain powers that be
has gained “too much freedom” and for this reason has
fallen from grace on the eve of the political battles of
1995-1996?

P.S. According to reports from independent, well-
informed sources, numerous memos on the television
subject have been circulating in top offices recently,
proposing that television be improved, reformed, and so
forth. In particular, Prime Minister Chernomyrdin
appeared to support proposals that originated with the
ARSTRC, namely that “Ostankino” be converted into a
joint-stock company and Russian Television be turned
into a state channel and fed from the budget in a normal
fashion. But there is another lobby on high, however,
and what in the end found its way onto the president's
desk was a memo drawn up in Ostankino’s corridors of
power proposing that the channels be merged. Yuriy
Baturin, presidential national security adviser, is known
to oppose these steps. Many ARSTRC staffers are cer-
tain that there is somebody in Yeltsin’s entourage who
has long and maliciously been trying to undermine their
company.

Duma Criticizes NATO Intervention in Yugoslavia

LD2511100094 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in
Russian 0852 GMT 25 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Ivan Novikov]

{[FBIS Translated Text] Moscow, 25 Nov (ITAR-
TASS)—The State Duma urged all sides involved in the
conflict in former Yugoslavia to cease fire immediately
and to return to political dialogue. This message is
contained in a statement adopted by the chamber at
today’s plenary sitting.

“The irresponsible behavior of the sides involved in the
conflict, the UN inaction during the offensive of the
Bosnian Government forces, and the United States’
unilateral decision to withdraw from enforcement of the
embargo on arms supplies to Bosnia-Herzegovina all
provoked an escalation in hostilities, which led to more
casualties,” the deputies state.

In the view of the State Duma, the latest bombings in
Serb Krajina and Bosnia cannot be justified. ““The
impression is that the United Nations is increasingly
being used as a cover for a NATO military intervention
in the civil war in former Yugoslavia, thus discrediting
the very idea of UN peacekeeping,” the statement says.

The State Duma urged the president and the Russian
Foreign Ministry to use, in the contact group for Yugo-
slavia and at the UN Security Council, all political and
diplomatic means available, including, if necessary, the
use of veto in the Security Council, in order to prevent
military intervention from abroad in former Yugoslavia.
The chamber deems it necessary, if any threat to the
personal safety of Russian servicemen emerges, to dis-
cuss the possibility of withdrawing the Russian peace-
keeping contingent from Yugoslavia.

Report on Kokoshin’s Briefing on Nuclear Forces

MK2311102094 Moscow NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA in

has already reported, the State Duma has held a discus-

Forces (SNF). A report on the subject was delivered by
First Deputy Defense Minister Andrey Kokoshin. The
hearings were also attended by Yuriy Glybin, first
deputy chairman of the State Defense Industry Com-
mittee; Colonel-General Igor Sergeyev, commander in
chief of the Strategic Missile Forces; Feliks Gromov,

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

and general directors of defense enterprises. They dis-

and of increasing their reliability and stability in combat.

According to the Russian Federation military doctrine,

The State Duma commission with the participation of

Academician Aleksey Yablokov held an enlarged
meeting today. The commission said the conclusions
drawn by the Komineft joint stock company , the Rus-

were not proved by available evidence (the assessment of
the area of the incident, video films, radio radar and
air-photo survey assessment).

the environmental disaster which had affected the
Pechora Region and to visit the Usinsk Region of Komi
in early December this year. By that time 52 kilometers
of a new section of the Vozey-Golovnye Sooruzheniya
oil pipeline must be commissioned.

The commission, headed by Chairman of the Environ-
meni?! Committee Mikhail Lemeshev, includes mem-
bers of the parliamentary committees on the environ-
ment, natural resources and security.

The oil spill on the oil pipeline near Usinsk took place in
August-September this year. The oil pipeline belongs to
the Komineft joint stock company. The Komineft
administration said at least 14,000 tonnes of oil had
been spilled as a result of the incident.

The Russian-U.S. Nordeco environmental joint venture
said proceeding from the air-photo survey that 103,000
tonnes of oil had been spilled. Nordeco representatives

Defense Committee Chairman on Duma Hearings

LD2411144494 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1144
GMT 24 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Anatoliy Yurkin]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow November 24 TASS—
Today’s hearings at the Russian State Duma on corrup-
tion in the western grouping of Russian troops in Ger-
many will not bring sensations, Sergey Yushenkov,
chairman of the committee for defense, told ITAR-TASS
in an exclusive interview.

Sergey Yushenkov believes that parliamentarians do not
have the whole volume of information. Besides, Yuriy
Boldyrev, former head of the control department of the
Russian president's administration, will not be present
at the hearings as he is on a business trip. Earlier Yuriy
Boldyrev prepared a special report on corruption and
could give additional facts of committed offenses.

According to Sergey Yushenkov, he does not expect
former commander of the western group of troops,
Colonel-General Matvey Burlakov, to be frank when he
begins giving explanations to parliamentarians today.

In reply to a question about the further investigation of
unlawful actions by officials from the Defense Ministry
in the western grouping of troops, Sergey Yushenkov
informed that the State Duma would consider this issue
again in December. By that time, he believes, parliamen-
tarians will manage to declassify many documents and
then it will be possible to have a comprehensive picture
of all that happened there.

The principle of distributing the military budget existing
in the Russian Defense Ministry, is creating conditions
for corruption, Sergey Yushenkov believes.

“Control over the Russian armed forces must be civil,
and the military budget—fully open as it is the practice
in other countries. Only in this event the army and the
navy will be purged from corruption,” the chairman of
the committee for defense pointed out.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination

32 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

Deputy Anatoliy Lukyanov (the Russian Way group)

Exchange. Following Gerashchenko’s resignation, the
president appointed Paramonova acting head of the
Central Bank.

Committee for the Budget, Taxes, Banks and Finances
passed a resolution which recommends that the State
Duma endorse Paramonova’s appointment to this post.

Before October 14, Paramonova was deputy leader of the
Central Bank and was in charge of budget planning and
the Central Bank’s relations with the Finance Ministry.

said that the draft budget “takes into account all revenue
sources and maximum results”.

State Duma Fails To Adopt Law on Referendum

LD2311215794 Moscow ITAR-TASS ‘World Service in
Russian 1730 GMT 23 Nov 94

[FBIS Translated Text] Moscow, [no date as received]
ITAR- TASS—The Duma was unable to adopt a draft
law “On a referendum of the Russian Federation,”
tabled by the president. For this constitutional draft law
to be adopted it would have been necessary for it to be
supported by two-thirds of the corps of deputies, or 300
parliamentarians. However, only 282 deputies voted for
it, with 30 against, and one abstention. A conciliation
commission has been set up that has been instructed to
prepare a new version of a draft law on a referendum.

Also today, the Duma adopted at a first reading a draft
law “On tabling changes and additions to the criminal
code of the RSFSR, and the Code of the RSFSR in
administrative infringements of the law,” connected
with the electoral process.

Adopts Law on Mass Media 23 Nov

LD2311165094 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1307
GMT 23 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Pavel Kuznetsov]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow November 23 TASS—
The State Duma, the lower house of the Russian parlia-
ment, on Wednesday adopted the law on state support
for the mass media and publishing houses upon the third
reading.

According to head of the Duma Information Committee
Mikhail Poltoranin, who presented the document, the
law will allow the mass media to get rid of poverty and to
become an efficient instrument to control state authori-
ties.

The document regulates tax, customs, currency and
other financial activities of the media and privatisation
of newspapers, magazines as well as printing houses.

The law provides for establishing a national fund of
development of the media. The fund is designed to
accumulate finances and invest them in the development
of the material and technical base of the mass media and
printing houses.

The fund will be a non-commercial organisation. The
Russian Federation’s supreme authorities are co-
founders of the fund.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

Under the law, the federal property needed for the
development of the media will be transferred to the fund,
except the property of editorial offices. The law also
stipulates that finances will be allocated to the fund for
projects to develop the media as part of federal budget
programmes.

A board of observers will be set up to control the
activities of the fund. The board will be formed by both
houses of the parliament, the president and the govern-
ment, each of them appointing five members to the body
for a three-year term.

November] evening by helicopter gunships of the Provi-
sional Council opposing Chechen President Dzhokhar
Dudayev, ITAR-TASS learnt from press secretary of the

a result of bombardments. Strikes were inflicted on
earlier reconnoitered targets, he stressed.

Council and are manned by Chechen crews.

Martagov noted that the air operation was carried out in
preparation for a capture of Groznyy. According to the
press secretary, opposition forces are reconnoitering
terrain and gradually occupying positions at the
approaches to the Chechen capital.

The opposition’s preparations are aimed at avoiding
casualties among civilians during an assault on Groznyy,
Martagov said.

Further on Helicopter Attack

LD2411075294 Moscow INTERFAX in English 0744
GMT 24 Nov 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Nine persons, including seven
civilians, were wounded as a result of an air strike
against the Shali tank regiment stationed 35 kilometers
away from Groznyy, the regiment commander, Isa
Daldakhov, told Interfax.

Daldakhov said a total of ten helicopters carrying Rus-
sian marks were engaged in the raid. Having encoun-
tered dense anti-aircraft defense, the helicopters flew
away to empty their cartridges at the Shali poultry
factory which was heavily damaged.

The shelling left two female factory employees and five
other civilians wounded, according to Daldakhov.

Two soldiers of the Shali regiment who were on duty at
the gate were also wounded. One of them is in a serious
condition. The hardware of the Shali regiment, the
government’s main strike force, was not damaged,
Daldakhov said.

from Nikolayev oblast that the Nikolayev nuclear power

Funding Crisis Could Cause Nuclear Station
Shutdown

MM2311143794 Moscow Ostankino Television First
Che «el Network in Russian 1800 GMT 19 Nov 94

[From the “Novosti” newscast—video report by Valeriy
Anuchin and Oleg Kul, from Kola nuclear power station]

{[FBIS Translated Excerpt] [Anuchin] A catastrophic
situation has developed at the Kola nuclear power sta-
tion which supplies electricity to all the major enter-
prises in Murmansk Oblast and Karelia. Two of the four
power units are already out of operation. Everything
points to the station’s grinding to a halt. In that case
giant enterprises like Severonikel, the Kandalaksha alu-
minum piant, the Appatit Association, and many Kare-
lian enterprises will come to a standstill. And the people
working at these enterprises will be left without the
means of subsistence.

Admittedly, the Murmansk Oblast Administration has
compiled a draft agreement in which it assumes the role
of coordinator and invites enterprises to make running
payments on time and in full for the electricity they use
and issue bills of exchange. But where are the enterprises
to take the money from, when most of their earnings go
primarily into the country’s budget and various federal
funds? There is no local organ which can change this
order.

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 35

And so a number of debtor-enterprises have gotten
together and taken out a credit of 30 billion rubles to
repay at least part of the debt. But this money is barely
enough to pay for the operation of the nuclear power
station for one month. [passage omitted]

Several Explosions Reported in Karelia

LD2211212194 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in
Russian 1352 GMT 22 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Vladimir Zlobin]}

{FBIS Translated Text] Petrozavodsk, 22 Nov—
Explosions have been rocking the capital of Karelia for
the last few days. It is not yet clear who is doing this and
why. Luckily, there are no casualties or severe damage.
The Petrozavodsk militia is looking for the unknown
plotters. An ITAR-TASS correspondent learned at the
Karelian Interior Ministry today that a special headquar-
ters to investigate the explosions had been set up and
criminal proceedings had been instituted.

Everything started on 18 November when a telephone
call to a militia station said that a design institute had
been mined. This proved a false alarm, however, and no
explosive device was found. But the next day a real blast
took place on the pedestrian bridge across the river
Lososinka in central Petrozavodsk. Yet another blast
took place near the town’s jail on the 2. November. And
the following day two other blasts went off near the
Railwaymen House of Culture, breaking windows in the
building and damaging the cars parked nearby.

The terrorists, however, did not stop at that. They made
another call and said that the stage of the same House of
Culture had been mined. There was a disco evening there
and the building was urgently evacuated but no bomb
was found. The last time the criminals phoned was on
the morning of 21 November. An unidentified person
said that the building of the Karelian pedagogical insti-
tute was mined. Lectures were urgently stopped but
nothing was found.

Krasnodar Mayor’s Removal Based on ‘Dubious’
Pretext

MM2311155394 Moscow KOMSOMOLSKAYA
PRAVDA in Russian 23 Nov 94 p 3

[Report by Irina Kiryanova under the “Provincial
Chronicle” rubric: “Yeltsin Deprives Krasnodar of a
Leader”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] Krasnodar—The November
elections for the Krasnodar City Duma and the kray
parliament had looked set to produce absolutely nothing
of any note. The only hitch consisted in the abundance of
candidates. However, we all know: If the action stops,
bring on the “deus ex machina.” On this occasion, the
role of the god was played by the Russian president
himself. On Friday, two days before the elections and, as
they say in such cases, “for no apparent reason” he
signed an edict removing one of the candidates from his
post—Krasnodar mayor Valeriy Samoylenko, to be pre-
cise. Thus the president interfered, and most unceremo-
niously, what is more, in what was an exclusively local
train of events. At the same time he left Krasnodar for
several days without a proper government, insofar as the

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

local soviets were dissolved in an equally cavalier
manner almost immediately after last year’s Moscow
drama.

Valeriy Samoylenko is an authoritative politician, and a
fairly popular one in the North Caucasus. He is president
of the Association of Cities of Southern Russia, in which,
incidentally, he managed to get Yerevan and Sukhumi
involved in the capacity of non-affiliated members.
Again, he is vice president of the Union of Russian
Cities. He became Krasnodar’s “top dog” some time ago
now. To a large extent, it is he who can take the credit for
the fact that Krasnodar survived August 1991 so well,
and celebrated its 200th anniversary peacefully last
October, despite the shooting in Moscow. And for the
fact that, amid all the cataclysms of recent years, there
have been no major economic or social failures here, nor
fatal interethnic clashes. And that is in spite of the highly
complex ethnic makeup of the Kuban and the extremely
suspicious attitude of the local population toward per-
sons of non-Slav nationality...

But what he really deserves to be remembered for is the
fact that, through his own determination and authority,
he brought about the Kuban capital’s unparalleled leap
to a completely new level of culture. In the last two or
three years the city has provided itself with a symphony
orchestra, an organ hall, a classical ballet, an opera
house, a conservatory faculty attached to the Academy of
Culture...

The official pretext for the dismissal is extremely
dubious. Apparently the mayor was failing to carry out
the president’s edicts and the government’s decrees.
Which ones exactly? In an interview for the city news-
paper Valeriy Samoylenko recalled just one instance of
disobedience: in a willful decision he banned the priva-
tization of bakeries. Which was probably a sensible
move.

Local analysts are inclined to take the view that the
president’s edict was a reflection of the rivalry between
two influential groupings which have formed of late in
the North Caucasus. One of them is based on the
Association of Cities of Southern Russia and led by
Samoylenko himself. The other is formed around a
similar association of regions of the North Caucasus and
gravitates toward Nikolay Yegorov, head of the Ministry
of Nationalities Affairs and Regional Policy, and
Vladimir Shumeyko.

We asked one Krasnodar wit, who was in a neighboring
republic when this bolt came down from the blue,
whether the president’s edict surprised him. The edict
does not surprise me, he replied, but the president does.

Incidentally, in last weekend’s City Duma election 10 of
the 27 candidates elected were “Zhirinovskiy men”’....

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

25 November 1994

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 37

had failed to anyhow react to the strikers’ demands to repay

[FBIS Translated Text] Washington—“Seventy seven

person who initiated the Ukrainian renaissance,” Bill
Clinton said turning personally to Leonid Kuchma.

Russian Paper Comments on Kuchma’s U.S. Visit

MM2311140594 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
23 Nov 94 p 3

{Commentary by Vladimir Nadein: “America Offers
Kuchma Friendship But No Checkbook”’}

{[FBIS Translated Text] Washington—A 30-gun salute,
the playing of the national anthems, and a ceremonial
march-past on the South Lawn of the White House
marked the opening of the state visit to the United States
by Leonid Kuchma, Ukraine’s second president. The
Ukrainian is only the fourth leader, after the emperor of
Japan, the president of South Africa, and the president
of Russia to whom official Washington has accorded
such an honor during Bill Clinton’s two years in office.

Hardly had the dense smoke above the guns dispersed
than the two presidents disappeared with their closest
advisers into a White House office to exchange opinions
and, most important, look one another in the eye and
work out where they stand.

The new Ukrainian president had spoken by phone with
Bill Clinton several times and they had exchanged let-
ters, but they had never met in person. Is Kuchma going

UKRAINE 39

to become “friend Leonid” along the lines of Russia’s
“friend Boris” or Germany’s “friend Helmut’?

Leonid Kuchma represents a country with a somber
present and an alarming future but a country which is
also moving increasingly rapidly to the fore in the list of
U.S. priorities. Ukraine presents a picture of inflation in
the past year at almost 1,090 percent, a 36-percent drop
in production in the same period, corruption and crime
rivaling Russia and Colombia, and a population that is
rapidly sinking into poverty—and all this is not even the
price of reforms. There are no reforms. The problem is
compounded by ossified economic forms and the active
reluctance (at least until very recently) of the power
structures in Ukraine to take the necessary steps toward
the free market.

However, Ukraine’s strategic position and its prominent
position in the system of former Soviet republics lead the
Americans to the conclusion that the collapse of inde-
pendent Ukraine would have a Chernobyl effect on
European stability.

As the president election in Russia draws closer,
prompting thoughts about the future of the fragile part-
nership between the former superpowers, U.S. policy
makers are increasingly turning their gaze toward
Ukraine. Of course, there is no question of playing the
“Ukraine card” against Russia.

After Leonid Kuchma’s election as Ukrainian president,
even those who advocated playing the Ukraine card fell
silent and waited for better times. And that despite the
fact that the administration’s critics were extremely
forthright until recently, demanding almost that Ukraine
be admitted into Nato.

Despite the differences of opinion with the White House
on a number of foreign policy problems, the new Repub-
lican majority in Congress is hardly going to oppose
support for Ukraine. However, the new congressional
leaders have promised to look more closely at whether
U.S. taxpayers’ money is being used to help crooks in the
government get rich or to help structural transforma-
tions.

The recent measures unveiled by Leonid Kuchma have
been given a rapturous reception in Washington.

As Nicholas Burns, the U.S. President's special assistant
for Russia, Ukraine, and Eurasia, stated recently, Leonid
Kuchma has undergone “a remarkable change—in the
twinkling of an eye. Our government was afraid that
Ukrainian statehood was in serious danger because of
the lack of economic reforms.”

The ratification of the Nuclear Nonproliferation Treaty
by the Supreme Rada [Council] removed some major
obstacles to economic aid for Ukraine. The way has been
opened for the United States, Canada, and the IMF to
keep their promises and issue $725 million in the final
quarter of this year for the stabilization of the Ukrainian
economy. A further $350 million will be allocated by the

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

40 UKRAINE

United States as part of a technical aid package. All of
this will make Ukraine the fourth-largest recipient of
U.S. aid after Israel, Egypt, and Russia.

U.S. diplomacy also takes the credit for settling, albeit
temporarily, Ukraine’s debts to Russia and Turkmeni-
stan. For Ukraine this problem is vital. It is not the
United States or Canada, much less the countries of the
European Union, which are adroitly avoiding giving aid
to the former “second among equals among free repub-
lics,”” but Russia and Turkmenistan that are today the
main donors to the Ukrainian economy. They are owed
more than $3 billion.

After a two-day break in Hawaii to recover from a
grueling nationwide tour on the eve of the elections and
get over the disappointment of a crushing defeat, Presi-
dent Clinton wil! be very sensitive and responsive in his
conversations with the new Ukrainian leader. However,
Ukraine is not going to get any special U.S. commitment
to support its independence and territorial inviolability
going beyond the scope of conventional guarantees to
countries party to the Nonproliferation Treaty. The size
of the aid package is not likely to be increased either.

Leonid Kuchma is not even slightly prey to the fond
hopes which his predecessor, Leonid Kravchuk, cher-
ished during his first visits to Washington. The United
States, for all its financial clout, has ceased to be a large,
open checkbook for the Ukrainian president. Despite
Kiev’s desperate need for foreign investments, reliable
trade and economic partnership with Russia is the key to
ending the crisis in Ukraine.

L. Kuchma knows that, so does B. Clinton. They also
know that B. Yeltsin knows it.

Foreign Minister Discusses Moscow-Kiev
Relations

MK2311132094 Moscow MOSKOVSKIYE NOVOSTI
in Russian No. 58, 20-27 Nov 94 p 12

[Interview with Ukraine Foreign Minister Hennadiy
Udovenko by Dmitriy Yakushkin under the “Diplo-
macy” rubric: “There Is Too Much That Ties Us to
Russia]

[FBIS Translated Excerpt} In the wake of the Ukrainian
parliament’s passing the decision on the accession to the
Nuclear Nonproliferation Treaty, one of the problems of
the republic’s relations with Russia and with the rest of
the world has been struck from the agenda. MOSK-
OVSKIYE NOVOSTI political observer Dmitriy
Yakushkin discusses other foreign policy aspects with
Ukraine Foreign Minister Hennadiy Udovenko.

{[Yakushkin] Sir, the impression is that the Russian-
Ukrainian talks are skidding. The dates of Boris Yeltsin’s
visit to Kiev are constantly being moved back.

{[Udovenko] It is journalists who, to a certain extent,
forced us to name these dates. We discussed the subject

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

with Andrey Kozyrev back in early September and came
to the conclusion that we should not commit ourselves to
any certain date.

There is now generally a proposal first to resolve the
navy and debt problems, and only after this sign the
treaty. There is also the opposite approach: Let us sign
the treaty first and then proceed to solve specific prob-
lems, concerning the navy again, on its basis.

[Yakushkin] To which line do you adhere?

[Udovenko] I believe there should first be a treaty
because otherwise we may be dragging our feet on it. On
the other hand, our talks on the remaining problems are
not going too badly. When there are no talks, everyone is
looking over their own shoulders, and that is too bad.

At the same time, I would like the Russian mentality
changed....

[Yakushkin] In what respect?

[Udovenko] One should get used to the fact that Ukraine
is an independent state. [passage omitted]

[Yakushkin] What was your concept of Ukrainian for-
eign policy when you took the ministerial post?

[Udovenko] We are not altering the foreign policy
course; it is the same as it was when formed by the
preceding Supreme Council when it was approving its
foundations. But I consider it necessary to add fresh
emphases in foreign policy. This platform of mine was
approved by parliament, which rather unanimously con-
firmed my candidacy.

[Yakushkin] What do you have in mind by “fresh
emphases?”

[Udovenko] There is a need for normalizing relations
with Russia, and very much has already been done in
this area.

[Yakushkin] Do you think these relations were “not
normal” until recently?

[Udovenko] They were steeped in ideology; may things
were perceived in our country as an attempt at dictate. If
up to now, three years after Ukraine and Russia became
independent states, there is no treaty between them, this
is hardly “normal.” The previous treaty—while it was
not too bad—was signed when there was still the Soviet
Union. Still unsettled is the Black Sea Fleet issue.
Separatist sentiments in Crimea are very strong, and we
are only asking for whatever guarantees of territorial
integrity to us. Thank God, President Yeltsin is talking
about this. [passage omitted]

The priority of our relations with Russia and the CIS
countries, however, should not be at the expense of
cooperation with the West. [passages omitted]

[Yakushkin] Do you feel pressure in appointing new
ambassadors of a independent Ukraine? Is this hard?

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-227
25 November 1994

[Udovenko] Selecting ambassadors is a constant head-
ache. We do not have the personnel basis that Russia
has. Therefore, we have had very many appointments for
political motives. They will be occurring also today; we
cannot get away from this, we do not work in a vacuum.
Unfortunately, we will not be able to fill all the vacancies
with career diplomats. We have decided on Ukraine’s
ambassador to the United States (he is Yuriy Shcherbak,
an epidemiologist, author, who was ambassador to
Israel—note by D. Ya.). Now Russia is on the agenda
[passage omitted] [interview ends]

P.S. The United States and Great Brit~in clearly wel-
come, via their ambassadors to Kiev, Ukraine’s acces-
sion to the Nonproliferation Treaty. Russian disarma-
ment experts have been more cautious. They believe in
particular that it has not been made clear in which
capacity Ukraine appears in the given case—as a nuclear
or a nuclear-free state. The preamble to the law on
Ukraine’s accession to the Nonproliferation Treaty con-
tains references to previous understandings on this
score. On the other hand, parliament has stated that the
nuclear arms are Ukraine’s property. The accession law
also enters into force after Ukraine receives security
guarantees, although a reverse procedure was originally
called for.

Crimeans Note ‘No Logic’ to Kiev Law Decision

MM2311143594 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
23 Nov 94 p 2

[Report by Nikolay Semena: “Crimea Depressed by
News from Kiev”}

UKRAINE 41

{[FBIS Translated Text] Simferopol—The Crimean
Supreme Soviet Presidium has examined the results of
two hearings: in the Ukrainian parliament “On the
Political-Legal Situation in the Crimea” and in the
Russian Federation State Duma “On Russian-Ukrainian
Relations.”

Supreme Soviet Deputy Chairman V. Mezhak, who
spoke at both the hearings and the State Duma session,
described the results of the Crimean deputies’ trip to
Moscow. He stressed that during the meetings Uk:aine’s
and Russia’s approaches to the conclusion of a wide-
ranging treaty on the Crimea’s participation in this
process were analyzed. He said that an accord was
reached on the creation of an off-site Russian Federation
consulate in the Crimea, on a mechanism for the people
of the Crimea to take up Russian Federation citizenship,
and on the creation of permanent State Duma and
Crimean parliamentary missions to the Crimean
Supreme Soviet and the State Duma respectively.
Progress was also made when it came to resolving the
issue of the Crimean ruble zone. Presidium members
approved the speech delivered at the State Duma hear-
ings by Admiral E. Baltin, commander of the Black Sea
Fleet.

However the Ukrainian Supreme Council decision to
abrogate around 10 Crimean laws and Supreme Soviet
decrees, as Presidium Member M. Bakharev said, left the
impression of being hasty and drastic. Perplexity was
voiced at the fact that before the session Ukrainian
deputies were talking about the discrepancies between
the Crimean and Ukrainian Constitutions, but at the last
minute the Crimean Constitution was left untouched
while Crimean rulings that had already become invalid
and were no longer in force were revoked wholesale. For
instance, the Declaration of State Sovereignty, as it
pointed out, was only in force until the adoption of a
Crimean Constitution. No act of independence was
adopted in the Crimea, but there was an Act on State
Autonomy, which also ceased to be valid with the
adoption of the Constitution.

Nor is it clear why Kiev revoked en masse even those
Crimean laws that do not contravene the law on the
demarcation of powers between Ukrainian and Crimean
state organs adopted by mutual consent of the two
supreme councils. What is the point in abrogating the
law on the 1994 budget when just over a month remains
until the end of its term, given, moreover, that the
mutual treaty on interbudgetary relations is in force?

Presidium Member S. Nikulin pointed to another incon-
gruity—the laws were abrogated not as laws, as should
have been the case in accordance with generally accepted
practice, but...as a list [perechen], a new word in law-
making activity. Presidium members noted that the
decision adopted in Kiev has no logic and in terms of
form and essence is purely political rather than legal.

Russian Duma Deputies Urge Compromise

LD2311200194 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1919
GMT 23 Nov 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Ivan Novikov]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow November 23 TASS—
Deputies of the State Duma expressed concern over the
way relations between Ukrainian and Crimean Parlia-
ments were developing on Wednesday.

The lower house of the Russian Parliament issued a
statement in connection with the Ukrainian Parlia-
ment’s decree “on fulfillment of the Ukrainian Parlia-
ment’s decree "on political and legal situation in the
autonomous Republic of Crimea.“

“We call upon (our counterparts), all citizens of Ukraine
to behave toward the hopes and problems of the
Republic of Crimea with the same amount of tolerance
and respect as Russia did toward establishment of the
independent Ukrainian state,” the statement reads.

Deputies of the State Duma believe that “the language of
ultimatums and blockade is the worst way to settle the
discords.” The fraternal peoples of Russia and Ukraine
have an interest in common which consists in protecting
the Crimea from any military pressure, because the
peninsula is overcrammed with weapons. The State
Duma is not indifferent to the destiny of the Crimea due
to the fact that any acceleration of tensions on the
Crimea echoes tn Russia.

“There are no issues in Ukrainian-Russian relations
which could not be solved by means of bilateral talks,”
the statement reads.

Unless a compromise agreement is reached in the Ukrai-
nian-Crimean conflict, it will be impossible to reach an
agreement on the future of the Black Sea navy, about its
anchorages and quarters, and, consequently, the signing
and ratification of Russian-Ukrainian treaty of friend-
ship, cooperation and partnership, the statement reads.

National Affairs

Kuchma Issues Reform, Anti-Monopoly Edict

AU2311143894 Kiev HOLOS UKRAYINY in Ukrainian
22 Nov 94 p 5

(“Ukrainian Presidential Edict on Measures To Ensure
the Fulfillment of Decisions on Reforming Ukraine’s
Economy” signed by President L. Kuchma on 18
November]

[FBIS Translated Text] Proceeding from the fact that
individual bodies of state executive power are blocking
Ukraine Cabinet of Ministers’ measures to reform the
economy and are setting high monopoly prices where
these prices should be formed independently by agree-
ment between producers and consumers, and proceeding
from the fact that this is making the population oppose
the implementation of radical economic reforms, I
resolve as follows:

1. To hold the leaders of ministries, other central bodies
of state executive power, the government of the Crimean
Autonomous Republic, and chairmen of oblast, Kiev,
and Sevastopol city councils of people’s deputies person-
ally answerable for failure to fulfill the Ukraine Cabinet
of Ministers decisions on implementing economic
reforms and for allowing departmental influence on the
setting of high monopoly prices and rates.

To determine that those officials who are responsible for
opposition to the Ukraine Cabinet of Ministers’s deci-
sions regarding reforms in the economy and for setting
high monopoly prices and rates must be punished in
accordance with the law to the extent that they might be
dismissed from their posts.

2. Ukraine’s Antimonopoly Committee, jointly with
Ukraine’s Ministry of Economy, must submit within one
month proposals on measures to restrict branch monop-
olism, in particular, to abolish those structures that,
through administrative influence, cause price increases
proceeding from their departmental interests and in
defiance of the citizens’ interests.

3. Ukraine’s Ministry of Economy, jointly with corre-
sponding ministries and other central bodies of state
power, with the participation of local bodies of power
and self-government, must submit by | January 1995
proposals to ensure prices are set for the main products
manufactured in Ukraine in taking into account market
demand, through organizing fairs and exchange trade
and creating other structures that will function on new
principles.

UKRAINE 43

4. Ukraine’s National Bank must submit by | January
1995 proposals for the sale of products on Ukraine’s
domestic market with payment exclusively in the
national currency.

[signed] Ukrainian President L. Kuchma
[dated] Kiev, 18 Nov 1994

Parliament Expects ‘Battle’ Over Power Bill

WS2411092494 Kiev INTELNEWS in English 0238
GMT 24 Nov 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Kiev, Nov. 23.-—President
Leonid Kuchma’s return home from the United States is
likely to be followed shortly by a sharp battle with
Parliament over his proposals for the division of govern-
ment power.

According to sources close to presidential advisor Vol-
odymyr Hryniov, Kuchma is likely as early as Monday or
Tuesday to submit to Parliament a bill greatly increasing
his own power at the expense of the legislative branch.

Ukraine’s government currently consists of a confusing
political patchwork in which presidential and govern-
mental decrees and parliamentary laws often contradict
each other or are ignored by local authorities.

“The draft foresees the transformation of Ukrainian
Parliament from an organ that has unlimited powers, as
it is today, into a European-style parliament, which
would function only as a legislative body,” said Hryniov.

According to the bill, the president will head the entire
executive branch, including the Cabinet of Ministers,
and will be solely responsible for appointing government
staff. The government appointed by the president is to be
approved by Parliament.

If Parliament rejects presidential proposals twice, the
president can order it dissolved, according to the bill,
which would also give Parliament the right to impeach
the president, but only under strictly specified condi-
tions.

The draft law also foresees that a prime minister will play
a different role, concentrating on organizing and con-
ducting the sessions of the Cabinet of Ministers, as well
as working on the directives and resolutions of his
immediate superior—the president.

“I believe the Kiev party branch will play a key role
during the first political test of our party—the struggle
for approval of the presidential bill division of powers, a
cornerstone of Kuchma’s reforms,” said Hryniov,
speaking to a Tuesday meeting of the Kiev branch of the
Inter-Regional Reform Bloc attended by over 200 dele-
gates.

Regarding the anticipated parliamentary reaction to the
law, Hryniov said “Parliament is not likely to give up on
its own most of its powers and concede to narrowing its
functions.” But the president is determined to push this

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

4c UKRAINE

law, and if Parliament rejects it, Kuchma may call for a
referendum on the issue, Hryniov said, echoing an
earlier statement by the president.

LD2311181094 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1559
